## J P M O R G A N

## Global Data Watch

Disinflation not likely to be sufficient for rate cuts by March

● PMIs underscore ongoing geographic and sectoral divergences

Geopolitics spark goods pricing inflation; Food prices falling

Next week: US Dec CPI (0.26% core); Nov IP (Ger, UK, Czk, Saf, Ind)

## It’s not soup yet

Central banks in the DM will not be ready to step back from their restrictive policy until they are convinced that core inflation rates are on a path to move sustainably below 3%. Inflation performance over the past six months has fueled optimism that we are on this path, with core inflation running at roughly 2%ar in both the US and the Euro area (Figure 1). This benign inflation backdrop has changed our thinking somewhat, as we have pulled forward our start of Fed easing and added another ECB cut this year. This comes alongside our recent upgrade to the likelihood of soft-landing. However, absent a growth threat, our central view is that it will take more time for central banks to reach this conclusion and look to midyear as the most likely time for DM central bank easing to begin (Figure 2).

It is natural to extrapolate the sharp and broad-based slide in global core CPI inflation over the past six months. But as we highlighted this week, the primary driver of this move—goods price deflation—looks to be abating. Combining this with fading technical distortions, we expect Euro area core inflation to rebound to a 3%ar in 1H24. In the US, where core CPI inflation is running a percentage point higher than core PCE inflation, fading goods price deflation should offset an expected moderation in shelter inflation, keeping core CPI inflation stable at 3%ar. The recent weakness in “supercore<sup>”</sup> PCE service price inflation is harder to assess. But with non-market prices playing an important role in its slide and the CPI counterpart much firmer, the Fed is likely to view the slide cautiously.

This week<sup>’</sup>s FOMC minutes suggest two judgments are important in a Fed forecast for 75bp of easing this year. First, “participants judged that the current stance of monetary policy was restrictive and appeared to be restraining economic activity and inflation.<sup>”</sup> Second, they expected a tight labor market to move closer to balance this year. In addition to demand restraint, participants see greater balance arising

as “higher labor force participation and immigration, with continued solid productivity growth (are) also supporting the productive capacity of the economy.<sup>”</sup>

US GDP growth is poised to moderate following last year<sup>’</sup>s 3% gain, but expectations for quicker and larger policy easing need to be tempered against a recent easing of financial conditions. At the same time, the rapid realized pace of supply growth (the labor force and productivity strengthened 1.3% and 2.2%, respectively, in 2023) is likely to cool. From a lofty 3.5% realized potential gain last year, we are forecasting US gains to slow to below 2% this year. This week<sup>’</sup>s employment report points in this direction and suggests the midyear drift upwards in the US unemployment rate has been arrested, despite moderating job gains.

By contrast, the Euro area stagnated last year with an even weaker supply-side performance that pushed its unemployment rate lower. For the ECB to hold its current restrictive stance in 1H24, it must be confident that the region will return to growth. This week brought some encouraging news on that front as the Euro area composite PMI was revised up in the final report for December, consolidating November<sup>’</sup>s increase. There was also better news on bank lending and German labor demand.

## Resilience doesn’t deliver convergence

The data flow reinforces our call for near-term growth resilience. We forecast just over 2%ar growth in 4Q23 and 1Q24, slightly below potential. This week<sup>’</sup>s J.P. Morgan Global PMI advanced further to end the year at a level consistent with 2.5%ar global GDP growth. Encouragingly, this move arrests a sharp five-month slide through October. A move up in the employment PMI bolsters this gain. Yet the growth gaps that opened up last year persist. Sectorally, the firming in the PMI owed entirely to a large gain in services that more than offset a drop back in the manufacturing PMI—its seventh month running below the 50-mark. Following a full year of stagnation, a modest recovery in factory output—to a roughly 1.5%ar—took hold in 2H23. But this lift appears to be narrowly linked to a rebound in Asia tech and a firming in the pace of stock building. With the new orders-to-inventory ratio of the PMIs still firmly in contractionary territory (Figure 3), this expansion does not have a firm footing. However, we take some comfort in the latest indicators that show final goods demand still rising—helped in part by signs of a pickup in global capex following a midyear stall.

Figure 3: Global manufacturing PMI and output
![](images/0f5379187c59e7b653bf004c54772676dd7d398290927d55bde3f6d95fd6140f.jpg)

Geographically, Western Europe continues to struggle, and China looks fragile. A surprisingly weak December ISM employment reading notwithstanding, the latest US business surveys overall underscore solid US momentum carrying into the new year. In Europe, an upward revision to the PMI is welcome but does more to remove downside risk than to change our view of an economy that is stagnating around the turn of the year. The main disappointment in this week<sup>’</sup>s data comes from China, where the more domestically-oriented NBS PMIs flagged ongoing weakness—despite improvement in the S&P Global PMI (which is in our global measure).

## Divergences in Asian manufacturing

We are also mindful of the ongoing divergences between tech and non-tech Asian manufacturing, as evident in the relative regional outperformance of tech-heavy Korea, Taiwan, and Singapore (Figure 4). Tech continues to lead production and shipments in Korea (benefiting from a strong DRAM lift), while broader non-tech production has weakened. Meanwhile, factory output is struggling in the more auto-dominated Japan, where production and exports recorded monthly declines through November. Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s export and industrial activity has shown steady sequential growth since midyear, but November export orders disappointed while this week<sup>’</sup>s December PMIs gave back months of recent gains. By contrast, the new orders PMIs from Korea, Vietnam, and Mainland China posted more constructive gains. On balance, we are upbeat on tech sector activity for 2024, owing to expected gains in demand for AI and high-performance computing and fading destocking pressures.

![](images/278c2f56a3e6f5f2e57f868565524b89ed68ae65c0746d5dec43e996e70f0fd0.jpg)

## Japan’s unhappy new year

While it is too early to fully assess, the disruptions to production from the devastating New Year<sup>’</sup>s Day earthquake in Japan appear relatively limited. However, negative impacts on sentiment risk exacerbating an already weak trend in manufacturing. Both industrial output and exports declined in November, dragged down by electronics and autos. Manufacturers<sup>’</sup> projections imply a sequential contraction into the new year; a pause in production by one large automaker (following a safety scandal) will further depress auto output. By contrast, domestic demand has held up: retail sales rose in November, and service sector activity and consumer sentiment remain supportive. This contrast, alongside continued wage hikes by Japanese corporates, has led us to expect only gradual BoJ policy adjustments over the course of this year. The past week<sup>’</sup>s events reinforce our existing forecast for the BoJ to exit NIRP no earlier than around the middle of this year.

## Red Sea disruptions reignite shipping woes

Following a series of attacks in the Red Sea, several major shipping companies have redirected vessels from the Suez Canal—which has seen a more than 20% drop in traffic since mid-December. Re-routing around Africa is adding upwards of 10 days to journeys between Europe and Asia, as well as significant increases in insurance and fuel costs. Panama Canal blockages have compounded the upward pressure on shipping costs. While these cost increases are coming off low levels, they will reinforce the fading of recent deflationary dynamics for goods prices. In addition to the recent rise in the NY Fed<sup>’</sup>s supply chain pressure index, a firming is also evident in the global PMI survey on output prices and delivery times in December.

## Food prices eased through December

Favorable supply conditions continue to limit food price pressures. The FAO food price index fell 1.5%m/m in December and was down 10%oya. Declines in sugar, vegetable oils, and meat more than offset weather-related increases in wheat prices (the first rise in four months) and upward pressure on rice prices from India<sup>’</sup>s export restrictions. We see global food CPI inflation (ex. China and Türkiye) continuing to hover around 3%-4%ar after a 3Q23 upswing to 5%. December food inflation looks to have slowed more than expected in the Euro area and Poland; food pressures also eased in EM Asia (namely Indonesia and Korea). The impact of El Niño on consumer food prices in the EM economies that have been most sensitive to severe episodes in the past (e.g., Colombia, Brazil, India, South Africa) remains relatively modest for now, although a greater impact could still be felt in 1H24.

## Türkiye: Higher for longer rates

Türkiye<sup>’</sup>s consumer prices rose 2.9% in December, in line with expectations, and CPI inflation increased to 64.8%oya (from 62%) on higher food and energy prices. Services inflation hit an all-time record of 90.7%oya, and core inflation increased to 70.6%oya (from 69.9%). Headline inflation is set to rise until May 2024 owing to higher-than-expected wage hikes and unfavorable base effects driven by natural gas. We thus revise up our year-end inflation forecast to 42%oya (from 40%). We continue to expect the CBRT to hike its policy rate by 250bp to 45% at this month<sup>’</sup>s MPC meeting, and believe its forward guidance will keep the door open for further hikes. Recent data reinforce our view that the CBRT should keep policy tight for longer, and we do not rule out further tightening depending on 1Q24 inflation. We continue to expect the CBRT to keep its policy rate at 45% (or higher) this year to rein in inflation.

## LatAm central banks treading carefully

The appreciation of most LatAm currencies in tandem with a recent easing of global financial conditions leaves the region<sup>’</sup>s central banks with a choice between direct FX intervention to contain the appreciation or using the opportunity to reduce policy rates further. In light of regional idiosyncrasies, we expect divergent policy decisions ahead. Colombia, which needs to maintain high real policy rates given persistent inflation and fiscal concerns, has already announced a reserve accumulation program. Chile had discontinued its mid-2023 FX intervention, but it would likely resume if current conditions persist. Mexico might also consider intervening, but the underlying financial cost and upcoming geopolitical events complicate the decision. Brazil has strayed from direct FX intervention and near term is likely to keep a more cautious approach. This reinforces the risk for lower rates by year-end, should our expectation for improving activity fail to materialize or services inflation decelerate more than expected.

# Nowcast of global growth: Downside risk to start 2024

This week we roll forward our global nowcaster tracking of real GDP growth to 1Q24. Early in a quarter, model forecasts for the unavailable data raise the nowcasts<sup>’</sup> standard errors.

We forecast that 1Q24 global real GDP will expand 2.3%ar, a tick up from 2.2%ar last quarter (Figure 1). We expect growth in the US to decelerate to 1.3%ar, but look for the Euro area to pick up from a 4Q stall to 0.5%ar. We think China will move sideways at 5.5%ar. Underlying these moves is a narrative of resilience, where easing inflation and solid labor markets promote continued consumer spending despite high interest rates. This week<sup>’</sup>s data support this story, suggesting solid momentum at year-end. The global all-industry PMI picked up 0.6pt on service sector strength, and this week<sup>’</sup>s US labor market report showed still-strong (albeit moderating) job gains.

Figure 1: J.P. Morgan global GDP
![](images/b751d7d6f59db04de2cb302043a590e5c2776f26c82d0813f8dd7c617e8a4881.jpg)

The top-down global nowcaster starts the 1Q24 global growth tracking exercise at 1.4%ar, suggesting 0.9%-pt of downside risk to our forecast (Figure 2). By contrast, the bottom-up nowcaster is 0.8%-pt above our forecast for the same country set. The nowcaster suggests upside risk to growth in China (+2.7%-pts) and to a lesser extent the US (+0.3%-pt), but downside risk in the Euro area (-0.4%-pt). The nowcasters are less accurate early in the quarter, using model estimates to fill out much of the quarter<sup>’</sup>s missing data. The top-down nowcaster has been under-predicting growth this expansion, likely owing to the strength of the less-represented service sector.

The release of the December PMIs dominated data news this week. The all-industry PMI gained 0.6pt to 51.0, a level suggesting slight upside risk to our current- and last-quarter global growth forecasts. Underlying this increase were diverging moves in services and manufacturing; services output gained nearly a full point, suggesting the post-pandemic normalization support to growth continued through year-end. Manufacturing, meanwhile, slipped 0.4pt to continue its recent trend of bouncing around low levels. This week<sup>’</sup>s update of our global manufacturing expectations index, however, provided some better news on the global goods sector, with a 0.2pt rise. Our MEI remains at a depressed level, but this second consecutive gain could indicate that global business confidence has found a bottom.

## J.P. Morgan Market Watch

## Bonds

Bonds rallied into year-end amid bullish Fed expectations after the Dec meeting and softer data, and saw a reversal since the start of the year amid a somewhat stronger data flow including Euro area PMIs. In the US, we unwound tactical shorts (UST Market Daily, Jan 4th) but are reluctant to add longs given aggressive pricing of Fed cuts, fair valuations, and our client survey pointing to more neutral positioning. Instead, we continue to hold 5s/30s steepeners as a medium-term bullish proxy.

In the Euro area, rates shed some of the extreme dovishness following upward revisions to PMIs and rebounding inflation. We keep a strategic long in 5Y Germany and turn tactically bullish on 1Yx1Y €STR. We stay constructive on intra-EMU spreads and hold long 5Y France vs. Germany. In the UK, strength in pay measures should keep the BoE on hold in 1Q24 and likely 2Q with a high bar for a cut in the next few months. We keep Feb/Mar MPC OIS steepeners.

In EM, on the strength into end-2023, we took profit on some bullish EM FX positions such as longs in BRL put spreads, though we stay OW Brazil local bonds (BRL: Hitting targets early, Jan 2<sup>nd</sup>). More broadly, we are OW local bond duration via Indonesia, Czechia, Poland, South Africa, Brazil, Peru and Uruguay, with partially offsetting UWs from Thailand and a modest short duration overlay (EM Fixed Income Focus, Dec 15<sup>th</sup>).

## Credit

HG bond spreads have widened slightly in the first week of 2024, unwinding some of the 6bp of tightening in December but not the 22bp of tightening in November. The recent weakness is understandable in the face of the very tight level at which spreads ended the year (113bp, 39bp tighter in 2023), the heavy \$57bn of supply that came this week, and the increase in geopolitical tensions in the Middle East. The dynamic of attractive yields helping to support tight spreads has been evident in the HG market for the past year, but the current level of spreads is still quite tight. Historically the excess return one earns 12 months forward when investing at these spread levels has been negative. We expect tight spread levels to persist (our YE24 spread forecast is 125bp), but spread volatility is likely to remain high given the tight valuation. Turning to supply, the first week of each year is usually heavy. Tight spreads and low(er) yields argue for front loading of supply this year, as does the election in 4Q. A macro contributor of the YTD widening has been a rethink of Fed expectations, with rate cut odds getting reduced (CMOS, Jan 5<sup>th</sup>). In 2023, we saw strong positive ratings momentum overall but slower into year-end.

Figure 1: Year-to-date returns %
![](images/1091c508f752b2fbf0a677c7201f9ec433ddc7b23f02cf1d4e84c86a605f5039.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan Global Economics

For US HY, default activity totaling \$1.0bn in December was the lightest since October 2022. In full-year 2023, 41 companies defaulted totaling \$58.3bn in bonds (\$27.5bn) and loans (\$30.7bn) and 47 companies completed a distressed exchange totaling \$25.4bn in bonds (\$10.1bn) and loans (\$15.3bn, record high). Including distressed exchanges, the par-weighted US high-yield bond and loan default rates decreased 4bp and increased 3bp m/m to 2.84% and 3.15%, respectively. For context, the 25-year average HY and LL default rates are 3.4% and 3.0%, respectively. Recovery rates for high-yield bonds ended 2023 at a 3-year low. Meanwhile, recovery rates for leveraged loans are at a record low due to the impact from the loan-only issuer base. Recoveries for HY bonds and Loans on an LTM basis are 32.8% and 38.3%, which compare to their 25yr and 24yr annual averages of 40.2% and 64.3%, respectively (Default Monitor, Jan 2<sup>nd</sup>).

In Europe, the buyer’s market is unlikely to last long, but risks abound. In our view, the technicals will improve by the middle of this month. We believe that the burst of issuance this week was mostly front-loaded refinancing, with little net supply to be printed. Nonetheless, we remain cautious as: i) spreads do not offer much compensation for downside risks; ii) shipping disruptions raise the threat of a third supply shock in almost as many years; and iii) central banks may disappoint against very aggressive policy rate expectations (European Credit Weekly, Jan 5<sup>th</sup>).

Thomas Salopek (1-212) 834-5476 thomas.salopek@jpmorgan.com

New issuance from EM Corporates ends 2023 at \$245bn after a quiet December. January is the busiest month for the new issue market with an average of \$55bn since 2018, and we expect an active pipeline given the conducive environment of positive sentiment and supportive technicals (EM Corporate Supply Technicals, Jan 3<sup>rd</sup>).

## Currencies

Uneasy transitions at the turn of the year. Inflation optimism is already well priced in the dollar, despite lingering uncertainties. The onus remains on the data to corroborate this narrative, which is why we see little value in chasing USD lower when growth and yields still remain challenged outside the US. Early Fed QT taper is more symbolic and not a material setback for the dollar. Our view that euro is better used as a funder remains unchanged, but for EUR/USD to weaken in line with our targets, US exceptionalism will need to become a dominant theme again.

On macro trade recommendations we take partial profits on SEK longs via GBP, and keep SEK longs vs CZK and EUR. We hold USD longs vs EUR and CAD in options; maintain AUD longs vs CAD & NZD in cash; and keep EUR/ JPY and USD/JPY shorts via options. We stay OW EM FX (FXMW, Jan 5<sup>th</sup>).

## Commodities

Oil demand likely grew in December at a slowest pace in nine months. Early observations of the oil demand in December 2023 suggest that world<sup>’</sup>s oil consumption growth will most likely undershoot our expectations by 200 kbd at 1.3 mbd YoY. Despite undershooting in December, global oil demand is still tracking a 1.8 mbd growth in 2023, in line with our published estimates (Oil Demand Tracker, Jan 5<sup>th</sup>).

The 2024 Commodities Index Rebalance is expected to drive buying in Natgas, grains and nickel, but moderate selling in WTI oil, precious metals and most other industrial metals. The new 2024 weights for both the BCOM Index and S&P GSCI Index were announced in 4Q23, but the actual rebalancing process will take place over the January 2024 roll period which begins on Monday, January 8. Relative to our December preview, estimated NYMEX Natural gas buying has reduced modestly to around 64,400 contracts (5% of total open interest) amid a late-year rebound in pricing. In metals, estimated selling in LME Aluminum & Zinc has been slightly boosted but still looks rather modest across base metals at around 1-2% of total open interest (ex-Nickel which is likely to see buying amounting to around 7% of total open interest). Precious metals still continue to face moderate selling over the rebalance (2024 Commodities Index Rebalance, Jan 5<sup>th</sup>).

## Not so fast: Global core CPI to rise 3% in 1H24

• After two years of 5%ar gains, global core inflation steps down to 3.3%ar in 2H23

• Goods deflation likely transitory as downward pressure on goods demand and input costs are fading

Service price disinflation to be limited by labor cost pressures and still solid sectoral demand growth

1H24 global core inflation likely to settle near 3%, which won’t resolve the immaculate disinflation debate

Just as a rising tide of growth pessimism took hold as we turned into 2023, a wave of inflation optimism is rising as we turn into 2024. Importantly, this optimism is not linked to growth concerns. Instead, global financial conditions are easing as confidence builds that inflation can fall further without a significant cost to output. Rates markets price a near complete return of inflation to target in the US and Western Europe over the coming two years (Figure 1). This view has been boosted by a shift in central bank guidance led by the US Federal Reserve. In its latest summary of economic projections, the FOMC median voter sees US inflation returning to target over 2024-25, alongside GDP growing close to trend, stable unemployment rates and 200bp of Fed easing.

Figure 1: 2y inflation swaps
![](images/20485b3a47c1835cb2cb39616f100b8d777f28a97e1c4d391e194987730eee7c.jpg)

A sharp and broad-based slide in core inflation during 2H23 contributes to this optimism; it sends an encouraging signal that much of the post-pandemic inflation spike represents a set of negative supply-side shocks now fading from the scene . It would be a mistake, however, to view this dynamic, which has pushed global core CPI inflation down nearly two percentage points over the past two quarters, as likely to generate a substantial additional inflation slide in 1H24 (Table 1 and 2). Core goods price inflation has been the dominant disinflation driver thus far, with prices declining at a 1%ar in the three months to November. However, after having falling sharply last year, supply chain pressure indicators have reversed in recent months, with a notable turn up in both air

and shipping freight costs. Alongside this move, the disinflationary impulse from a full year<sup>’</sup>s contraction in manufacturing looks set to be abating as global factory output returned to growth in 2H23.

Table 1: Core CPI

Global service price inflation also moved lower in 2H23 but recent months have seen core services inflation move sideways at a roughly 4.5%ar. In contrast to the goods sector, service sector demand growth remained strong over the past year and the unwind of dislocations that have tightened labor markets and pushed wage inflation higher has not normalized. Against this backdrop, we forecast only a moderate slowing in service price inflation in 1H24 to a 4%ar. A firming in core goods price inflation to a 0.8%ar is expected to keep global core inflation at a 3%ar.

Complicating the 1H24 outlook are significant recent downside surprises in Euro area HICP and US core PCE service price inflation. In the Euro area we believe the recent slide in service price inflation, that pushed core HICP inflation below a 1%ar in the three months to November, reflects technical distortions set to unwind this quarter and push core inflation rates back to a 3%ar in 1H24. In the US, core PCE inflation rose at a modest 2.2%ar over the past three months, well below the 3.4%ar rise in core CPI (Figure 2). While a difference in the treatment of shelter prices explains part of this gap, PCE inflation has also been depressed by unusual weakness in non-market based prices which more likely represents noise than signal.

Source: J.P. Morgan

Figure 2: US service prices % change over 3 months, saar
![](images/a4da4634cd3598f240167c5850a5393ee9292992ed4318bcc0455cdad7e1992d.jpg)

An expected stall in the pace of disinflation during 1H24, does not diminish the importance of the unwind of most of the 2021-2023 inflation spike. From the perspective of our scenario assessment, it increases the odds of a shift to less restrictive monetary policy stances later this year. Alongside recent growth resilience and easier financial conditions, the prospect of central bank easing in 1H24 has raised our assessment of the probability of a soft-landing outcomes to roughly equal to a <sup>‘</sup>boil the frog<sup>’</sup> outcome in which sustained restrictive policy stances gradually undermine private sector health. That said, a forecast of firming core goods and sticky service price inflation will likely limit the size of 1H24 easing relative to current market pricing, absent a more tangible threat to growth.

## Global shocks rise and lower all boats

After hovering at a 5%ar for two full years, global core inflation took a significant step down to a 3.3%ar in 2H23 . This move aligned with our forecast at the start of last year. And it confirms that a significant portion of the 2021-2023 global inflation spike was linked to negative supply shocks related to the COVID pandemic and Russia<sup>’</sup>s invasion of Ukraine that are now fading from the scene. That global inflation has risen sharply since the end of 2019 while GDP fell well short of its potential path is consistent with a negative shock. While global GDP has grown cumulatively 1%-pt less than its pre-pandemic potential pace since 4Q19, core CPI inflation stood 2%-pts higher in 2023 at a 4% rate.

The synchronization of inflation dynamics across countries (both up and down) provides additional evidence of the dominant role of negative supply shocks (Figure 3). The COVID and Russia invasion shocks have generated widely divergent growth outcomes, reflecting differences in economic structures, policy choices and private sector behavior. But inflation performance does not reflect these differences. With the notable exception of China, inflation moved sharply higher across the globe, both headline and core, over 2021-22, before taking a significant but partial step down in 2023.

Figure 3: Core CPI and GDP deviation from potential, 4Q23 Core CPI, %oya
![](images/c9e2d2884994f9d9f1717e4c610a4377854dbc1c972e4d307237e5043d4912a0.jpg)
Source: National sources, J.P. Morgan. Details on request. GDP dev from potential, %

## Core goods prices are falling …

The dominant driver of the 2H23 core inflation slide has been goods prices. After rising 4% in the year ending in May, core goods prices moved into deflation last quarter, with prices declining at a 1%ar over the three months through November (Figure 4). Consistent with the broad theme of powerful global supply shocks, the move up and down in global goods price inflation has been accompanied by sympathetic swings in the FRBNY global supply chain pressure index (Figure 5). With supply chain dislocations and 2022 commodity price spikes now removed, 2023 saw sharp falls in global transportation costs and a stabilization in intermediate goods prices. Notwithstanding considerable US volatility, this up and down pattern is reflected across the globe.

Figure 4: Core goods CPI
![](images/c5f590847d2b562d562c389357b0b9f725ab47ae7845e2c65a8ff63e83e0713a.jpg)

Figure 5: Global supply chain prices and core goods CPI Index
![](images/49c52c8dd2531ce029fa98868ef8e3eca137c82e1164ddc2e0bdc74961b6ad9a.jpg)

The sharp up and down swings in goods price inflation over 2020-23 was reinforced by sharp swings in global goods sector demand. Upward pressure on prices over 2020-22 was magnified by a rotation in global demand towards, tech, construction, and consumer goods in the early stage of the pandemic recovery. This impulse has faded as a broader normalization in mobility shifted the composition of demand and global interest rates moved higher. Global manufacturing output declined in the year through mid-2023 and sluggish goods demand combined with fading supply-chain pressures to reinforce goods price disinflation (Figure 6).

Figure 6: Global real GDP, decomposed relative to potential Index, 4Q19=100
![](images/80c44925b6959cb3e120ea2fc5adffaeea4195a74de0545591275eefca8f2d49.jpg)

## … but deflationary impulses are fading

While a resurgence of goods price pressure is not in the offing, there is good reason to see the current phase of goods price deflation ending soon. This is the message from the FRBNY supply chain pressure index which has turned upwards last quarter, partly reflecting the reemergence of upward pressure on global transportation costs. A similar firming is also evident in the global PMI survey on output prices and delivery times (Figure 7).

Figure 7: Global manufacturing PMI, delivery times and prices DI, sa; both scales
![](images/7f2092759362d29f2a58c814a183ff5ee06ccfef886fb36d9e434ee97e2ba5ab.jpg)

There is also a shift in the global business cycle underway which looks likely to reduce downward pressure on goods prices. Since mid-year a modest recovery in global industry has been underway in which output is on track to rise at a 2%ar in 2H23 (Figure 8). The profile of demand is more mixed as a rebound in tech and a modest recovery in consumer goods spending has been accompanied by a softening in equipment demand.

Figure 8: Global goods supply and demand %3m, saar; CapexNow and retail sales exclude China
![](images/c6b83a8fe7f09d7cee049397015a60624c4a880bc62ade602d0b2d4ef3d93921.jpg)

It is too early to gauge how significant a positive impulse these forces will place on core goods prices. Our model which forecasts global core goods price inflation based on supply chain pressures and GDP growth, suggests last quarter will mark a bottom for core goods price inflation and that core goods prices will return to modest positive gains during 1H24 (Figure 9).

Figure 9: Global core goods CPI %q/q, saar, shaded area is J.P. Morgan forecast
![](images/a8bf3c1fae724eeb0ce0961b1d7369c9fda01686b307300082893fbfd41d7e56.jpg)

## Service price inflation is sticky …

Supply-shocks pushed service price inflation higher over 2021-23 as well. In addition to the pass-through of the rising prices commodities and manufactured goods inputs, the pandemic shock produced a tightening in labor markets despite shortfalls in growth (Figure 10).

Figure 11: Global CPI - ex Chn & Tur
Figure 10: Global GDP and unemployment rate (ex. China) %-pt deviation, both scales
![](images/713ede204aa699c689c879989c8fe444304698c4d01c9a6ec46d235f6e464abc.jpg)

Across most of the globe, labor costs gains were pushed materially higher with DM wage inflation running above 4% in 2023, more than 2%-pts above its 2016-19 pace. Global service price inflation reached a peak 6%ar pace in early 2023. While it moved lower in 2H23, global service price inflation appears to have stabilized in recent months at close to a 4.5%ar, roughly twice its pre-pandemic pace (Figure 11). Across countries the Euro area has been a notable outlier as the latest three months has delivered a material deceleration in service price inflation (Figure 12).

![](images/590042cb100d7c31fedddd69f2fd848f0f86c23335dc9ac338ff681507ebe4e5.jpg)

Figure 12: Core service prices % change over 3 months, saar
![](images/dd8bd9c09d3d35f2f4f53b1de05722a2fbee7166f425d3ce61dc68618e5368df.jpg)

## … and looks likely to remain so

Give the sympathetic movement in core goods and service price inflation since 2019, it would seem reasonable to anticipate that core service price inflation follows the sharp path of goods prices lower. However, the pass-through from goods to service price inflation has historically been very modest during periods in which the two sectors face different macroeconomic impulses. As we moved through the past year, the divergences between these sectors has widened in a manner that does not suggest that service price inflation will follow the lead from goods prices. Specifically,

Service sector demand remains firm. Last year<sup>’</sup>s surprising resilience in global growth reflected sustained above-trend gains in service sector GDP which grew 3% over the past four quarters (Figure 13). Because of this sustained strong demand impulse, most indicators suggest that global labor markets are materially tighter than their pre-pandemic position.

Figure 13: Global service sector GDP %ch over 2q, saar
![](images/a1c150664b44c508cf9f5ad86b5f57ac78c4bae4f1de334af630db0c9c841568.jpg)

Labor costs remain elevated. Against this backdrop there is no broad-based parallel to the normalization in supply-chains for the service sector. Although job vacancies have moved lower, they remain elevated. The case for service price inflation following a sharp disinflationary path is limited as labor markets remain tight and costs continue to rise at an elevated pace (Figure 14). What<sup>’</sup>s more, this year<sup>’</sup>s GDP growth surprises reflect the strength in service sector activity which has sustained above-trend pace for most of the pace three years.

Figure 14: Wages and core services prices, Developed Markets %oya,
![](images/869d7c55bffa44d8a55754f8b560dd94ace569a41d018a46a8d8aff3ace8bc19.jpg)

## Euro area core momentum to rebound

Complicating this assessment of core inflation are the recent downside surprises in Euro area core service price inflation , which has pushed core HICP to a 0.9%ar in the three months to November, and US core PCE inflation, which rose at a 2.2%ar, well below the 3.4%ar rise in core CPI. These surprises play a significant role in market pricing of early central bank easing.

Rates markets currently price more than 150bp of ECB easing next year (with 75bp already in 1H24), a development that in part reflects sustained stagnant growth. A decline in the December flash PMI reinforces growth pessimism and the case for early easing should build quickly if growth weakness translates into labor market easing. However, we attribute an important element of the recent core inflation slide to technical distortions. The December flash HICP should see this distortion begin to unwind. Combined with a normalization in the Germany VAT tax at the start of the year, we anticipate core HICP to rebound to a 3.5%ar this quarter, before settling below 3% (Figure 15).

Figure 15: Euro area core HICP % change, saar
![](images/56ed4a7a4a37589b5331cab24c490633be91ad72ad66d093b24a8f8238b190f7.jpg)

## Dueling US supercore measures

In the US, core PCE is the Fed<sup>’</sup>s preferred inflation measure and its sharp deceleration is a key factor behind the recent FOMC pivot. While part of the ongoing gap between the two US core inflation measures reflects the different weights attributed to shelter cost, the recent widening in the gap is centrally linked to the divergent trajectory in non-shelter services (“supercore<sup>”</sup>). While the CPI measure has accelerated to a faster than 5%ar in the past three months, supercore PCE has remained below a 3%ar (Figure 16).

![](images/92631bc82fbe41d7363eee179a0fbb4195cd0e97e8da83a32642f9e5cd66e129.jpg)

Wide differences in the scope of these two measures make comparisons difficult, but it is important to recognize that the scope of the PCE measure is far broader and includes several non-market-based price estimates. From our perspective, caution should be taken in placing significant weight on any specific price basket that moves out of line with the broader range of inflation signals. If we are right, upcoming reports are expected to show still tight labor markets and firm wage gains alongside some firming in supercore PCE inflation.

## United States

• Job growth was solid in December (216,000) with unemployment rate holding at 3.7%

• FOMC minutes suggested rate cuts could come later this year

• We continue to think first Fed cut occurs in June

• December CPI forecast: headline: 0.2%, core 0.3%

We continue to think that the FOMC will start lowering rates in June, with the news from the past week supporting the idea that a rate cut could come fairly soon, but probably not as early as the first quarter. Most importantly, the December employment report showed solid job growth continuing through the fourth quarter, with a still-tight labor market generating upward pressure on wages. The broad trend in employment growth (and aggregate hours growth and average hourly earnings growth) still appears to be moderating over time, but so far this looks like a shift to a more balanced labor market following the severe effects of the pandemic, and not a labor market that has particularly weak demand for workers. Several other labor market reports echoed this message lately (e.g., job openings continued to trend lower into November but remain higher than pre-pandemic norms), although there was a curiously downbeat reading on employment in the ISM services survey.

Signs of a more balanced labor market and reduced inflation pressures are leading to speculation about when the FOMC will start to lower rates. The minutes to the December FOMC meeting didn<sup>’</sup>t surprise in a meaningful way and didn<sup>’</sup>t suggest that rate cuts were imminent. They confirmed that the Committee sees rates “at or near<sup>”</sup> the peak for the cycle. While “almost all participants<sup>”</sup> thought the improved inflation outlook means rate cuts this year, they are still far from guessing when those cuts might start. The minutes did suggest that we should start to see more discussion of plans for the balance sheet soon, and we think this could start to occur at the late-January meeting.

Inflation remains a key issue for policymakers and next week<sup>’</sup>s December CPI report will be important to watch. We forecast that the headline CPI will be up 0.2%, with a 0.26% gain for the core index. While this wouldn<sup>’</sup>t be a particularly soft print for the core measure, we believe the related yearago rate will continue to moderate (moving from 4.0%oya in November to 3.8% in December) and we expect additional cooling over time. This upcoming CPI report likely will be the last main economic release ahead of the late-January FOMC meeting, but there will be plenty of other news before that meeting as well.

## Job growth still solid

Nonfarm employment beat expectations in December, increasing 216,000, although after downward revisions of 71,000 to prior months the level of employment came in close to what was anticipated. There were also mixed messages from the other details of the report. In the establishment survey, average hourly earnings increased a solid 0.4%, but the average workweek ticked down to 34.3 hours. In the household survey, the unemployment rate held unchanged at a low 3.7%, but the labor force participation rate slumped threetenths to 62.5% and the volatile household measure of employment plunged 683,000.

Figure 1: Nonfarm employment and average hourly earnings 000s, sa, 3m avg. change %
![](images/cdd99d9d1dd0dd6a2cef7802af328144f5f7d67185ace057731e89f98aae1fae.jpg)

Stepping back, labor demand has been gradually cooling. Job growth averaged 165,000 in the fourth quarter, the weakest quarter of the expansion though still above what<sup>’</sup>s required to meet the growth in the population (Figure 1). Similarly, wages grew at a 3.7% annual rate last quarter, the weakest pace since late 2020 though still probably a little too hot from a labor cost and inflation perspective.

Figure 2: Job openings and layoffs
![](images/c3b5d5d94bdd76bd4201ee2474e806180c087e2f68c0431847d1326cd1acb4e6.jpg)

The more lagging JOLTS data also suggest that labor demand is cooling but remaining solid. Job openings continued their recent downward trend into November. But while the number of job openings has fallen by more than 3mn since the alltime high reported for March 2022, the November level of openings was still up by more than 1mn from the strongest month of 2019 (Figure 2). While hiring has slowed lately,

firms don<sup>’</sup>t appear to be opting for many layoffs. And low levels of jobless claims filings reported through much of December echo this message from the November JOLTS report.

## Fed minutes don’t signal timing of a cut

We believe the apparent resilience of the labor market should dial down expectations of a Fed rate cut in 1Q, and we still look for easing to begin at the June meeting. The minutes to the December 12-13 FOMC meeting didn<sup>’</sup>t surprise in a big way and didn<sup>’</sup>t suggest that a rate cut is imminent. The minutes rather confirmed that the Committee now sees rates “at or near<sup>”</sup> the peak for the cycle, and this was reflected in the revised forward guidance. However, that guidance still left intact “the possibility of further increases<sup>”</sup> if inflation were to surprise on the upside. “Almost all participants<sup>”</sup> noted that the improved inflation outlook meant rate cuts in <sup>’</sup>24, though they are still very far from getting into the game of guessing when those cuts may start. It was also noted the outlook for these cuts was “associated with an unusually elevated degree of uncertainty<sup>”</sup> (the minutes rarely note an unusually low degree of uncertainty).

The Committee generally views the risks to inflation as still skewed to the upside, though by not as much as in prior meetings. Some sources of lingering upside risk include the recent easing in financial conditions and global factors could end or reverse the easing in goods prices. On growth there was the contending concern that the Committee could find that policy is ultimately overly restrictive, which could be compounded by slower foreign growth and tighter bank credit, particularly for small businesses. Both the Committee and the staff saw a tough year ahead for CRE.

The minutes record that “several<sup>”</sup> participants anticipate slowing the pace of balance sheet runoff before stopping that runoff. It was also noted that these participants thought the technical discussion should begin so that the public has appropriate advance notice of the plan. We suspect this means that we could see a fuller discussion of potential balance sheet plans in the minutes to the next meeting, which takes place later this month.

## 4Q GDP still looking modest

We have long been anticipating a clear slowing in GDP growth in the fourth quarter following the very strong third quarter of last year and we continue to calibrate our estimate for 4Q as we get more related information over time. There were some upside surprises this past week, but we continue to see some downside risk to our 2.0% real GDP growth forecast for 4Q23. We should also note that much of the slowing in growth we expect between the third and fourth quarters is

related to inventories. Inventories added 1.3%-pts to GDP growth in 3Q but we think they could subtract about a percentage point (or more) from GDP growth in 4Q. That said, there are limited related hard data in hand at this point. For domestic final sales growth, we expect growth to moderate between these quarters, but we do not look for a particularly drastic step down.

On the whole, the November construction report came out above expectations, with a somewhat disappointing change reported for November (0.4%) but upward revisions to earlier figures (mainly in October). Private residential spending led the way in November, with a 1.1% monthly increase that month, and generally has been climbing at a solid pace in recent months (Figure 3).

Figure 3: Main categories of construction spending
![](images/a47e488a7ae745adabe3ac9dcc3bc03e12ff1132a50b921d8b962e0930751268.jpg)

Auto sales also surprised favorably, rising 3.2% to 15.8mn saar in December. This marked one of the strongest months for sales in over two years but sales continue to run below the norms from pre-pandemic years. We think that improvements on the inventory front could help sales climb over time. While inventories generally have been trending higher lately, there was a decline in inventories reported for December.

We estimate that the nominal trade balance widened from -\$64.3bn in October to -\$64.5bn in November, with exports falling 2.2% and imports declining 1.7%. The advance trade report already showed widening in the nominal goods deficit in November with drops in both exports (-3.6%) and imports (-2.1%) during the month. Factoring in price data reported separately, we also expect to see drops in exports and imports in volume terms in November. For the nominal services data, we think that the growth trend for exports will remain firmer than that of imports in November, with exports rising 0.7% and imports moving up 0.2%.

We forecast that initial jobless claims increased 3,000 to 205,000 during the week ending January 6. The claims data can be noisy from week to week and we think that the drop in filings reported for the week ending December 30 will be partially reversed in the upcoming report. But we believe filings will stay low and remain close to their recent average.

We believe the consumer price index (CPI) rose 0.2% in December. This would be the firmest monthly change since September, as we think that the recent drop in energy prices is now behind us and we look for an unchanged energy CPI in December (in seasonally adjusted terms, despite prices moving down before seasonal adjustment). We also think that food prices in the CPI rose 0.2% in December, in line with the recent upward trend. And away from food and energy, we believe that the core CPI rose 0.26% in December, which would not be a particularly soft print but it would keep the year-ago change for this measure moderating over time (moving from 4.0%oya in November to 3.8% in December).

We look for mixed changes across the main core CPI components. We think that the rent measures will keep climbing at a solid, though decelerating, pace in December—we forecast that tenants<sup>’</sup> rent rose 0.34% in December while owners equivalent rent increased 0.33%. We also look for another solid gain for medical care prices in the CPI (0.5%) as we think that health insurance prices will keep increasing in December because of the BLS<sup>’</sup>s estimation techniques. We also look for a 0.9% increase in new vehicle prices based on our analysis of related industry figures.

But we look for declines in prices across several other core CPI categories. Industry figures tied to used vehicles point to recent weakening, and we forecast a 1.6% drop in the used vehicle CPI for December. And we also think that lodging prices will continue their recent downward trend, falling 0.3% in December. In other travel-related news, we forecast a 0.5% decline for public transportation prices in December. Communication prices generally keep moving down over time, and we expect a 0.1% decline for the related CPI measure in December. Apparel prices in the CPI also generally have

moved lower lately through some noisy monthly prices, and we forecast a 0.1% decline for December.

We estimate that the producer price index (PPI) rose 0.3% in December. Energy prices fell across October and November but we see some recent firming in related measures (like natural gas prices) and we forecast a 2.1% move up for the energy PPI to be reported for December. Food prices in the PPI have been alternating solid gains with soft monthly changes lately, and we look for a continuation of that trend with a flat print in December to follow the 0.6% November increase. Away from food and energy, we forecast that the core PPI rose 0.2% in December. This would be a step up from the flat readings reported for October and November but would still keep the broad trend for the core PPI moderating over time.

Nominal construction spending rose 0.4% in November. While this was somewhat short of expectations in terms of the November change, there were upward revisions to earlier figures (mainly in October). And on the whole, the news from the latest report was favorable with respect to our 4Q GDP tracking, but we still see some downside risk to our forecast for 2.0% real GDP growth that quarter.

In terms of the main details within the construction report, private residential spending rose 1.1% in November and generally has been increasing at a solid pace in recent months. The trend for private nonresidential spending has been softer lately, including a 0.2% move up reported for November. Public construction spending, meanwhile, dropped 0.7% in November, but this decline came after a run of monthly gains extending back into late 2022, and the earlier recent public spending data also were boosted meaningfully by revisions in the November report.

rate (%)</td><td>39</td><td>3.8</td><td>3.7</td><td></td><td>38 3.7</td></tr><tr><td>Participation rate (%)</td><td>62.7</td><td></td><td>62.8</td><td></td><td>62.5</td></tr></table>

Nonfarm employment beat expectations in December, increasing 216,000, although after downward revisions of 71,000 to prior months the level of employment came in close to what was anticipated. There were also mixed messages from the other details of the report. In the establishment survey, average hourly earnings increased a solid 0.4%, but the average workweek ticked down to 34.3 hours. In the household survey, the unemployment rate held unchanged at a low 3.7%, but the labor force participation rate slumped three tenths to 62.5% and the volatile household measure of employment plunged 683,000. Stepping back, labor demand has been gradually cooling. Job growth averaged 165,000 in the fourth quarter, the weakest quarter of the expansion though still above what<sup>’</sup>s required to meet the growth in the population. Similarly, wages grew at a 3.7% annual rate last quarter, the weakest pace since late 2020 though still probably a little too hot from a labor cost and inflation perspective. We believe the apparent resilience of the labor market should dial down expectations of a Fed rate cut in 1Q, and we still look for easing to begin at the June meeting. We continue to track 4Q annualized real GDP growth around 2.0% and look for the economy and the labor market to continue to slow heading into 2024.

In the establishment survey government employment growth was strong again, up another 52,000 last month, mostly at the local level. The 164,000 gain in private employment was anchored by private education and health services, up 74,000, and leisure and hospitality, up 40,000. Temp help was weak again last month, down another 33,000. Revisions continue to be biased lower, and October was the 9<sup>th</sup> month out of 10 last year to ultimately revise down. While the average workweek for all workers has bounced between 33.3 and 33.4 hours for most of last year, the workweek for production and supervisory workers moved down to 33.7 hours last month, the lowest of the expansion. With modest upward revisions to prior months, average hourly earnings ticked up to 4.1% on a yearago basis. Earnings for production and supervisory workers were up 0.3% last month, and year-ago earnings growth for these workers slipped to 4.3%. Aggregate hours worked increased at a modest 0.8% rate last quarter and total labor income grew at a 4.5% rate. The latter figure is the second lowest of the expansion though still pretty decent—particularly with core prices now only increasing at around a 2% rate.

Turning to the household survey, the large 683,000 decline in the volatile household measure of employment was the biggest decline of the expansion, and the payroll-concept adjusted measure was down an even larger 753,000. The 1.8 million year-ago increase in household employment is now running 1.0 million below the establishment survey measure. The decline in the participation rate was experienced by both men and women, by young, prime-age, and older workers, and by all racial groups. It<sup>’</sup>s hard to pinpoint the cause of this decline but it could reflect a little giveback from earlier outperformance. The setback in participation pulled the employmentto-population ratio down three tenths to 60.1%, the lowest reading of the year. An increase in the ranks of those working part time but who want full-time jobs caused the U-6 measure of labor market underutilization to tick up to 7.1%.

## ISM services survey (Jan 5)

<table><tr><td colspan="3">Sa</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Oct</td><td>Nov Dec 53.0</td></tr><tr><td>Headline composite Business activity</td><td>51.8</td><td>52.7 55.1</td></tr><tr><td>New orders</td><td>54.1</td><td>55.5</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>55.5 50.2</td><td>52.8 43.3</td></tr><tr><td>Employment Prices</td><td>58.6</td><td>50.7 58.3</td></tr><tr><td></td><td></td><td>57.4</td></tr></table>

The headline composite for the ISM services survey fell from 52.7 in November to 50.6 in December. The December print disappointed expectations and marked the lowest level reported since May. But somewhat curiously, much of the weakening in the December report was due to the employment index—which dropped from 50.7 in November to 43.3— despite many other reports pointing to relatively more upbeat conditions in the labor market as of late. In more favorable news from the ISM report, the business activity index moved up from 55.1 in November to 56.6 in December.

## Euro Area

• Upwardly revised PMI consolidates November<sup>’</sup>s gain

• Level remains low but concern about momentum has reduced

• Euro area inflation picked up in December, but by less than expected

ECB likely to remain cautious about near-term rate cuts

This week has brought some encouraging news on growth and a downside surprise on inflation. The composite PMI was revised up in the final report, removing entirely the decline that had initially been reported for December. As a result, there was no payback after a large gain in November. The level is of course still low, consistent with stagnant growth, but concerns about momentum have been allayed somewhat. Other data were more mixed: bank lending to the real economy picked up in November while a plunge in German retail sales, which is notoriously noisy, looks set to wipe out what would have been a decent increase at the Euro area level. But, overall, this week<sup>’</sup>s growth data were encouraging.

On inflation, the downside surprise was unlikely large enough to materially change the rates outlook. Headline inflation rose, reflecting a base effect in Germany, while core inflation eased. But, services inflation remained at 4.0%oya, which will keep the ECB focused on wages. We continue to think that the ECB will assess inflation, wages and growth data as a package, and that a pickup in growth is a key part of the ECB<sup>’</sup>s current caution about near-term rate cuts. At the same time, we also still think that the momentum in the inflation data will remain sufficiently above 2% for a while and that the ECB will want to wait for greater clarity on wages before having enough confidence to cut rates. We can see the ECB cutting earlier than our September call, but a cut before June likely requires an outright deterioration in growth from here combined with some labour market cracks. In this context, the German labour market report actually showed some improvement this week.

## Euro area inflation below expectation

Euro area headline inflation rose 0.5%-pt to 2.9%oya in this week<sup>’</sup>s flash report for December, in large part due to a base effect in German energy inflation. Core inflation eased 0.2%-pt to 3.6%oya, whereas we had expected a stable outturn. This is harder to interpret as the limited details do not yet allow two key distortions to be assessed. First, a post-pandemic rebound in the weight on package holiday prices is distorting the profile this year, distorting cheaper holiday months lower (e.g. November) and boosting school holiday periods (e.g. December). Second, the price collection methodology

for German package holidays was changed this year, which is changing the seasonal profile. The second effect may have worked against the first.

The bottom line is that core prices rose 0.22%m/m according to the ECB<sup>’</sup>s seasonally adjusted data, reflecting equal gains in core goods (0.23%m/m) and services (0.22%m/m). Annualizing this, core prices increased at a 2.7% pace in December. That is still solid and broadly in line with the trend before November. But, November itself was weak, with core prices falling outright on the ECB<sup>’</sup>s measure (Figure 1). Hence, there is a question about how to adjust December and how to average recent months. The message on core goods is clearest: it picked up after having declined outright by 0.1%m/m in the last three months. The message on services is less clear as it includes package holidays. Our inclination is to not change our assumption for the next few months and to wait for the final report for a fuller assessment. In any case, we still think that services inflation is underpinned by solid wage growth.

Figure 1: Euro area HICP core prices
![](images/e8999eb26545ab4344826d52ecdfcde6250979ee911ccf142e2970be2ced3f58.jpg)

## Euro area PMI revised up

After an encouraging 1.1pt increase in November, the flash Euro area PMI report showed a 0.6pt decline in December. This decline has been completely revised away in the final print. Upward revisions were broad-based by country and by component. This still leaves the PMI at a weak level, consistent with stagnant growth (Figure 2). But, at least, the question about a renewed intensification of the weakness has been removed. The revised composite employment index is now reported to have been stable, the new orders index improved and the future output index continued to recover (+1.6pt). The future output index remains higher than the current output index. Overall, this is an encouraging release, although the PMI will need to rise further to validate our forecast for modest growth. We also note that the ECB<sup>’</sup>s growth forecast is more upbeat than ours.

Figure 2: Tracking Euro area GDP growth with the PMI %q/q saar, model with dummies for 4Q08, 1Q09 and 3Q10-4Q12
![](images/3abcc32d5e15f711dc0f68b83f68de93cd7812a105c97f29ada100be00e911f3.jpg)

## Bank lending perks up

Bank loan growth perked up in November. Adjusted for loan sales and securitisations, loans to nonfinancial corporates jumped €20bn (0.4%m/m) and loans to households rose by a smaller €6bn (0.1%m/m) (Figure 3). The increase for households was also less broad-based by country. Nevertheless, November looks firm from a loan creation perspective. Of course, the data are noisy and therefore it will be important to see if the pickup is maintained in the next 1-2 months. An improvement would be positive in a broad sense, but we note also that the role of credit at this stage in the business cycle is not that clear-cut, as both corporates and households have good income/saving positions. In any case, the pickup in bank lending is noteworthy as lending rates are still moving higher.

Figure 3: Euro area bank lending to real economy €bn, monthly flows, adj. for loan sales/securitizations
![](images/d961c1f555bf415efbeae46a6a8d372b7a3cfca75d3212fc22165079c68136a1.jpg)

## A mixed set of German data

This week has brought a mixed set of German data. The German composite PMI was also revised up. But, in terms of manufacturing, German car production declined in December. Using the seasonal and calendar factors from the IP data, the VDA data suggest a 4%m/m decline in car production (Figure 4). This still suggests a very strong gain of 50%q/q saar in 4Q23, but creates a weaker trajectory into 1Q24. Also, it leaves car production still below the pre-pandemic level.

There have been some production interruptions recently and December can be affected by the timing of the holidays. As a result, it will be important to see the January data.

Figure 4: German motor vehicle production
![](images/e40f5c3e5f21ebc36c4a95dd350649f990d6622e3592fa9b1b20c5384b9b59eb.jpg)

Similarly, German retail sales plunged 2.5%m/m in November. These data are notoriously noisy and the timing of seasonal sales has been changing in recent years. For both reasons, it is too early to draw strong conclusions, especially as the outlook for real disposable incomes is improving. But, the report was nevertheless disappointing. It also contrasted with a labor market report that showed increased support for households. In particular, employment posted a second consecutive increase in November (Figure 5). Unemployment also rose by less than in recent months and job vacancies posted a first increase in many months. This turn is still quite tentative as the PMI employment index remains at a level that points to stagnant employment. Nevertheless, it is remarkable just how resilient the German labor market has been given the weakness of GDP over the past year. In our view, this reflects the high level of labor shortages reported by many companies and to a lesser extent some weakness in the workweek.

Week of January 8 - 12

Output and surveys

<table><tr><td colspan="2">European Commission survey</td><td rowspan="2">Sep</td><td rowspan="2">Oct</td><td rowspan="2">Nov</td><td rowspan="2">Dec</td></tr><tr><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Mon</td><td>Euro area</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Jan 8 11:00am</td><td>% balance of responses, sa</td><td></td><td>93.5</td><td>93.8</td><td>94.2</td></tr><tr><td rowspan="5"></td><td>Economic confidence</td><td>93.4 -8.9</td><td>-9.2</td><td>-9.5</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Manufacturing confidence</td><td>-5.8</td><td>-5.5</td><td>-4.8</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Construction confidence</td><td>-5.7</td><td>-7.4</td><td>-7.0</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Retail confidence</td><td>4.2</td><td>4.6</td><td>4.9</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Service confidence</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Consumer confidence</td><td>-17.7</td><td>-17.8</td><td>-16.9</td><td>-15.1</td></tr></table>

We expect Euro area economic confidence to have increased modestly in December. Consumer confidence looks to have increased solidly according to the flash report and this will help to lift the aggregate. This improvement could reflect the turn in real incomes given the solid momentum in wages and falling inflation rates. Indicators for business confidence are less clear-cut, but also point to a possible improvement: the composite PMI was stable in December but the future output index did increase significantly. Despite the modest increase we have pencilled in, we put more weight on the PMI, which remains at a level consistent with stagnant growth.

<table><tr><td></td><td>Manufacturing orders</td><td>Aug</td><td>Sep</td><td>Oct</td><td>Nov</td></tr><tr><td>Mon</td><td>Germany</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Jan 8</td><td>Volumes, sa</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>8:00am</td><td>Total (%m/m)</td><td>1.9</td><td>0.7</td><td>-3.7</td><td>2.0</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>%oya</td><td>-6.4</td><td>-3.5</td><td>-7.2</td><td></td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Total ex. bulk orders (%m/m)</td><td>2.0</td><td>-2.2</td><td>0.7</td><td>-0.3</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>%oya</td><td>-8.2</td><td>-8.5</td><td>-6.6</td><td></td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Domestic (%m/m)</td><td>2.3</td><td>-5.7</td><td>2.4</td><td></td></tr><tr><td></td><td>%oya</td><td>-4.8</td><td>-10.9</td><td>-7.3</td><td></td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Foreign (%m/m)</td><td>1.6</td><td>5.0</td><td>-7.6</td><td></td></tr><tr><td></td><td>%oya</td><td>-7.4</td><td>1.6</td><td>-7.1</td><td></td></tr></table>

German industrial orders remain weak, but the 3.7%m/m fall in October was largely due to bulk orders. On an ex-bulk basis, there was an increase of 0.7%m/m and the level was broadly in line with the 3Q23 average. In fact, ex-bulk orders have been broadly stable over the past eight months. While this is encouraging, it is still at odds with with the new orders index in the manufacturing PMI. This PMI indicator has increased by 10pts in recent months, but was still at a low 41.5 in December. That points to further weakness in ex-bulk orders. We have therefore pencilled in a small decline.

<table><tr><td colspan="2"></td><td rowspan="2">Aug</td><td rowspan="2">Sep</td><td rowspan="2">Oct</td><td rowspan="2">Nov</td></tr><tr><td>Tue</td><td>Germany</td></tr><tr><td>Jan 9</td><td>Industry incl constr (%m/m, sa)</td><td>-0.1</td><td>-1.3</td><td>-0.4</td><td>0.3</td></tr><tr><td>8:00am</td><td>%oya, sa</td><td>-1.5</td><td>-3.8</td><td>-3.4</td><td></td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Industry ex constr (%m/m, sa)</td><td>0.5</td><td>-1.8</td><td>-0.1</td><td>0.2</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Manufacturing (%m/m, sa)</td><td>0.6</td><td>-1.7</td><td>-0.5</td><td>0.3</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Construction (%m/m, sa)</td><td>-2.8</td><td>1.5</td><td>-2.2</td><td>1.0</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Energy (%m/m, sa)</td><td>-5.7</td><td>-2.4</td><td>10.6</td><td>-2.0</td></tr></table>

German IP disappointed in October, despite the recent stability in ex-bulk orders and the relative resilience in factory sales. In particular, the October level was around 5% annualized below 3Q23. The VDA car production data have not shown an increase for November and the business surveys are still weak despite their recent improvement. For these reasons, it is unclear that IP will improve much in November. The small increase we have pencilled in would leave IP tracking a decline of around 5%ar in 4Q23. This points to a modest GDP contraction, which we have in the forecast for Germany.

National data suggest two things. First, that October will get revised up to 0.3%mm and, second, that November will see a decline of 0.2%m/m. The latter owes a lot to a surprisingly weak 2.5%m/m plunge in Germany, where the data are notoriously volatile and prone to revision. The pattern of seasonal spending has changed in recent years and with increased prominence of early discounting events in November. This makes the November decline all the more surprising. Nevertheless, we think that conditions for households have improved recently and note that consumer confidence has

moved higher again. As a result, we still think that consumer spending is returning to growth.

<table><tr><td></td><td>Unemployment</td><td>Aug</td><td>Sep</td><td>Oct</td><td>Nov</td></tr><tr><td>Tue</td><td>Euro area</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Jan 9</td><td>Harmonized measure (Eurostat)</td><td></td><td>6.5</td><td>6.5</td><td>6.5</td></tr><tr><td>11:00am</td><td>Unemployment rate (%, sa)</td><td>6.5</td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Unemployment (ch m/m, 000s)</td><td>-114.0</td><td>58.0</td><td>20.0</td><td>30.0</td></tr></table>

The number of unemployed has increased over the past two months and we have pencilled in a further increase. But, this is unlikely to push the unemployment rate up at this stage. German unemployment stabilized in November and three smaller countries were also stable overall. As a result, our forecast allows some modest increases elsewhere and reflects that the PMI employment index has continued to move lower (before stabilizing in December). But, the PMI does still point to some modest job creation and therefore the increase in unemployment would only reflect a labor force effect. In this sense, the labor market remains stable.

## Japan

• Past week<sup>’</sup>s events reinforce our view of BoJ gradualism

• Watching for earthquake spillovers even as initial supply-chain disruption appears limited

• Consumer confidence rises to post-pandemic high through year-end

• Next week: Tokyo Dec global core CPI up 0.1%m/m sa; Nov wage growth firms

It is too early to make a detailed assessment of the macro impact of the devastating earthquake that struck Japan on New Year's Day. But while the direct impact on nationwide production and activity will be limited compared to the 2011 Great East Japan earthquake, there are two points worth making at this juncture. First, although supply chain disruption appears relatively limited, the negative impact on sentiment risks exacerbating what is an already weak trend in Japan<sup>’</sup>s manufacturing sector. Second, our baseline has always been for BoJ policy adjustments to take place gradually over the course of this year; the past week's events reinforce our view of gradualism, suggesting that an exit from negative interest policy will take place only towards the middle of this year at the earliest, in line with our existing forecast.

In this context, the incoming data over the holiday period have continued to paint a picture of a manufacturing sector struggling to kick into higher gear. Japan<sup>’</sup>s production and exports recorded monthly declines through November, with falls in production of electronics and autos dragging output down through late 2023. Manufacturers' projections imply a sequential trend contraction into the New Year, and a large auto-maker's decision to pause production on the back of a safety scandal will further depress auto output.

But in contrast to a struggling manufacturing sector, domestic demand—particularly service sector activity—has held up. Retail sales rose solidly through November, and consumer sentiment remained relatively upbeat through December. The comparative resilience of domestic activity, alongside continued wage hikes being announced by Japanese corporates, should keep the BoJ on its medium-term path of gradual policy normalization.

## Consumer confidence up through year-end

Consumer confidence edged up in December, with a broadbased rise in indicators lifting the headline sentiment index 1.1 points to 37.2. This marks the highest reading since end-2021, and the largest increase in eight months, reflecting an improvement in consumers<sup>’</sup> perceptions across the majority of indicators. Consumers<sup>’</sup> assessment of their overall livelihood led the increase, with the sub-index up 1.6 points, its largest jump since April. Sustained labor market tightness, continued pressure for higher nominal wage growth, and accumulated household savings look to have bolstered consumer sentiment through year-end (Figures 1 and 2).

Consumer inflation expectations also ticked up. The balance of respondents expecting annual inflation rates to rise was up around a percentage point, leaving the seasonally adjusted net share at 90%. This is still a little off the peak earlier in 2023, but represents a material rise over the 2013-2020 average of around 75% (Figure 3).

## Canada

• Employment was flat in December

• Wage growth remained heated

• First Bank of Canada cut expected in 2Q24

The labor market cooled more than expected in December coming in essentially flat and missing our expectations. A static December followed a gain north of 29,000 in November. The unemployment rate held at 5.8% as we expected as the participation rate fell in December to 65.4%. However, the pace of wage growth on an annual basis re-accelerated last month. Average hourly earnings jumped to 5.7%oya (permanent employees) in December, after increases of 5.0%oya in October and November. While employment growth was weaker than expected, the Bank of Canada is focused on wage growth. Wage growth is still quite strong at 5.6%,saar 3mma and a key underlying factor in services inflation and manufacturers<sup>’</sup> costs. While run rates have moderated somewhat since a post-pandemic peak north of 9.0% in September, the most recent pace of wage growth still does not support a near-term pivot to easier policy by the Bank of Canada. Looking at the Summary of Deliberations of the December meeting, there was nothing in the review that indicated the Bank is ready to cut rates soon. We are expecting wage growth to moderate. We expect the first policy rate cut in 2Q24.

C\$/hr, sa, dashed line represents pre-pandemic trend
Figure 1: Average hourly earnings
![](images/434dca678480181401a753868bbb92f178a5e8527d8e6a2be3a22bd73bfc9190.jpg)

On average, job gains over 2H23 are still firmly above 20,000, entirely service sector work and largely full time. The industries that benefited the most were professional services and “other<sup>”</sup> services. Health care and education, categories where it<sup>’</sup>s likely that backfilling continued to dominate, were also solid. The principle laggards in the past six months— manufacturing, construction, wholesale and retail trade— align with what might be expected in an evolving cyclical slowdown. Natural resources job growth held in but generally at a subdued pace. In terms of labor market slack, the numbers of prime age long-term unemployed and involuntary part time have been creeping up through 2023 but remain well below pre-pandemic levels.

## Brothers grim

Business surveys continue to point to an economic landscape/ environment where slower economic activity prevails whether it<sup>’</sup>s on the consumer side or the business side. December<sup>’</sup>s S&P Global manufacturing PMI was disappointing. The further slide in the index in December just confirms that the signal about manufacturing output remains negative as the index has been notably below 50 for five months. And the new orders-to-inventory PMI ratio was the weakest since the height of the pandemic at 0.84, which hints at continued subdued production ahead. December was the second straight month of improvement in the future output PMI, making for a notable divergence. Higher interest rates look to be weighing on capex generally. In the monthly data on real manufacturing shipments through October, both durable and non-durable shipments generally rolled over in 2H23 but the downturn in durables looks more significant. The volatility in durables production has been sharper in recent quarters, likely related to the atypical swings in auto production. The downtrend in the composite PMI in 2H23 underscores the deceleration in activity from the solid 2.0%ar average pace in 1H23.

Figure 2: S&P Global manufacturing PMI: Output
![](images/e683b74786e6898816d77f41c151cf1a15be226b9965b2ed2beae3618d0ebf99.jpg)
Source: S&P Global, J.P. Morgan

While the CFIB<sup>’</sup>s Business Barometer index of small business sentiment improved a bit looking 12 months ahead and 3 months ahead, both levels remained extremely low in December. A floor under sentiment was maintained by a recovery in small business sentiment in the energy-based provinces. The number of small businesses that expect business to be stronger or somewhat stronger in the the next 12 months is close to the record low reached during the peak of the GFC. Heightened concerns over insufficient domestic demand are apparent. Despite the downbeat views, small firms are still expecting to maintain or increase full-time employment levels in the next three or four months. Predictions on average price increase plans over the next 12 months remained unchanged at 3.1%, the third consecutive month of stability, while average wage growth plans dropped slightly to 2.5%.
