## Mexico

Banxico minutes delivered further signs of gradual easing ahead

• We still expect the first cut in March, with rates at 9.75% by year-end

• Disinflation<sup>’</sup>s final stretch will prove lengthy; core stubbornness won’t go easily

• Next week<sup>’</sup>s IP

The minutes related to the last monetary policy decision of December 2023 were consistent with the statement itself, in which the board decided to retake a vigilant/cautious stance on the back of the complex inflation dynamics and acknowledging the uncertainty embedded in core inflation—particularly services. Still, it is evident that most members are preparing to discuss the appropriate time to start cutting rates in 1H24 given the degree of tightness and the disinflation gains evidenced since 2022. We still expect the first 25bp cut in March, reaching 9.75% by year-end.

As we expected, there were no dissenting voices as compared to the November minutes when Ms. Espinosa delivered a strong hawkish message intended to underscore the importance of maintaining a vigilant tone and emphasizing there were no conditions to start discussing cuts soon. Her message has clearly resonated and confirmed that the board is well aware of the challenges, and the fact that upside risks remain in place, which will be the main hurdle to opening the door for an outright easing cycle. We expect a pause in June, followed by back-to-back cuts in 2H24.

Figure 1: Core inflation expectations - Banxico survey %oya, average value
![](images/12832a88e903ed627c4cea11602ebb1a72c53bf788dc3916a15c7edf480153c5.jpg)

Looking at the details of the discussion, three members still argue the need to remain firm given the challenges ahead, with demand-side pressures expected to abound in the context of a positive output gap, a tight labor market, and considering fiscal policy pressures; however even some of these members (the neutral-to-hawkish members) seem well aware that the degree of tightness warrants a looser stance, particularly the one that has talked openly about “fine-tune actions<sup>”</sup>. Another member said that “current conditions are less adverse<sup>”</sup> and underscored improving inflation expectations (Figure 1).

On the other hand, the two somewhat dovish members acknowledged upside risks to inflation but believed that the disinflation process has advanced notably. The only outright hawkish board member in the minutes called for flexibility in the forthcoming decisions, suggesting she/he is aware that cuts are coming soon.

Interestingly, one of the neutral-to-hawkish board members emphasized that a tight stance is warranted, and that it is necessary to maintain the real ex-ante rate between 7% and 7.5%, but also acknowledged the importance to go ahead given the increases in the real rate since the hiking cycle ended—a message echoed by one of the dovish governors.

All in, we expect the discussion of cuts to start in February and the first cut in March, which also suggests the balance of risks to inflation will need to change some in the first meeting of the year. Discussion on the currency and the FOMC was scattered, and is expected to gain traction as geopolitical events intensify and the Fed cuts are openly discussed.

## CPI: Disinflation final stretch to prove long

Headline and core inflation have both come down a long way from their post-pandemic highs, but we expect getting them back within target will prove tough, at the very least. Next week<sup>’</sup>s December CPI report should be a case in point. We look for headline inflation to loop back up to 4.6%oya from 4.4% a month before, while core inflation remains uncomfortably high at 5.2%. Headline inflation could dip below 4% sometime this year, but core inflation is unlikely to, which should prove troublesome—we have shown headline inflation tends to converge to core, not the other way around.

Against this backdrop, momentum in core price gains is not yielding; we expect core prices to rise 0.4%m/m, sa again, consistent with 5%-ish inflation on an annualized basis. What is concerning is that sustained, strong momentum in core price gains is happening despite the downward thrust from unwinding supply shocks and a strong currency. Put differently, core services inflation remains extremely high and its momentum concerning.

We look for service-sector prices to rise 0.5%m/m, sa, or 5.8% on a 3m3m annualized basis (Figure 2). This would almost match the highest rate seen over the past two decades. Furthermore, we expect prices of services ex. housing and education to have increased at a 7.3%3m/3m annualized runrate in December. Part of this reflects, in our view, administrative cost increases in airfares, but even after stripping out tourism from the equation the picture remains troublesome.

The combination of tight labor markets, rapidly rising real wages, robust consumption, and a rotation toward services consumption underpins this unfavorable trend. Our concern is that as the tailwind to disinflation from nonfood goods ebbs, core inflation will likely settle above 4%3m/3m, saar from now through the middle of 2025, a potentially uncomfortable fact for Banxico (see section above).

![](images/fe57e77d14592b4ace95f9a6888d5e23c30f3cb9965561eeb9fe11de58d29319.jpg)

## IP motors higher, but not on manufacturing

We expect more of the same in next week<sup>’</sup>s November IP print. We look for a solid 0.7%m/m, sa gain in industrial output that would set it on track to post yet another strong 5.5%ar gain last quarter. Manufacturing likely failed to break out of its multi-month lull (Figure 3), even if we expect a 0.5% monthly gain driven by the auto sector—in turn a function of union strikes in the US auto sector coming to an end in October. But beyond that, manufacturing indicators remain soft.

The December PMIs and business surveys offer hope of a pickup, but we remain skeptical about a sustained rebound at this stage. Of note, last week we cut 4Q23 GDP to 3% from 3.5% on disappointing news early last quarter.

Figure 3:Manufacturing and construction Index, sa (both axes)
![](images/34ec72518dcc09f38cfe499dbc1469e74ac556af89d9c7c6039dbab498dadde5.jpg)

By contrast, we look for construction to deliver a 2.1%m/m, sa gain on the heels of a roughly 4% jump in October, extending the sector<sup>’</sup>s impressive momentum. Survey data suggest that construction put in place remained on an uptrend both in

November and December, and that sentiment among businesses in this industry remains very upbeat. Some slowdown in construction output looks likely this year as big infrastructure projects from the government come to an end. But we trust that the private sector will help cushion the hit from lower public spending, particularly as private spending remains robust, at least in 1Q24. This would be most visible in construction spending in industrial real estate, as has so far been the case.

## Brazil

• Data point to broadly unchanged scenario

• Positive surprise was stronger FDI in November...

• ...while credit behaved in line with recent evidence

• Final 2023 CPI to be next week<sup>’</sup>s main highlight

Financial conditions have improved for major economies over the past several weeks—a move also noticeable across Brazilian assets. Recent idiosyncratic developments, such as December<sup>’</sup>s higher-than-expected CPI preview, were not enough to stop the good momentum. The approval of a balanced budget after much speculation on a possible target change for 2024, as well as of other pending measures in Congress that aim to increase revenues structurally also didn<sup>’</sup>t hurt. In our view, this scenario is consistent with BCB<sup>’</sup>s most recent message, as the board signaled that the domestic scenario is evolving broadly as expected and was seemingly inclined to avoid volatility at the turn of this year.

As we await indicators for the last two months of the year, we begin this year filling in some of 2023<sup>’</sup>s missing pieces. This week BCB and IBGE kicked off January by releasing November data on the external sector, credit, industrial production, and fiscal accounts. According to BCB, credit slowed once again, though with improving financial conditions, while the stability of the current account deficit as share of GDP was offset by a very strong FDI flow. Fiscal accounts revealed that regional governments had a smaller-than-expected surplus, while the overall financing needs pushed net public debt to 59.5% of GDP by the end of the month. Industrial production, the first hard data for activity in this period, surprised two-tenths to the upside growing 0.5% in the month on the back of strong mining results.

Taken together, these new data points do not change our views on the outlook for the economy for 2024. Our scenario points to accelerating marginal growth in the near term as easier financial conditions continue to kick in, gradually slowing inflation amid a still tight labor market. On monetary policy we see ample space for keeping the 50bp in coming meetings and a final 25bp cut bringing the Selic rate to 9.5% by mid-year. The balance of risks for the policy rate is asymmetrically biased to lower terminal rates, in our view, especially if economic growth fails to accelerate in the near term, rendering our ever-watched nowcaster even more important (Figure 1). So, as we did this week, we will spend the following ones splitting our attention between incoming 2023 data and the first 2024 signs.

Figure 1: Brazil 4Q nowcaster
![](images/2ddaa5f5d15db5e272d02442e2931d78d61e5bf4003470f7d4b031411011c258.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan

## IP: booming mining!

Industrial production grew 0.5%m/m, sa in November, the fourth positive result in a row, and IP is now running at 1.6%3m/3m. Mining remains an outperformer and grew 14.5%oya, against a 0.9% contraction in Manufacturing (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Industrial production
![](images/013db994571d18b18a4cddac6adc843f0df32eb14616a94c1512204b7dae7e34.jpg)

## Fiscal: federal transfers are not enough

The general government posted a BRL37.3bn primary deficit in November, with regional governments contributing with a BRL2.0bn surplus (Figure 3). Financing needs partially mitigated the improvement seen due to new GDP numbers, with gross and net public increasing 0.1 and 0.3pp to 73.8% and 59.5% of GDP.

Figure 3: Fiscal balance
![](images/3b84b62178d4676aaf3eb2147092b5a85a48b2c828dbd88b864aa2a17e47b0b9.jpg)

Given December<sup>’</sup>s settlement of 0.9% of GDP in unpaid court-ordered debt, we look for a 2023 deficit of 2.2% of GDP, to be followed by 0.9% in 2024. The risks to the latter are skewed towards better results due to the finance minister<sup>’</sup>s ongoing revenue agenda, but we see three reasons to hold our view for a moderate deficit this year.

## Credit: beauty in the breakdown

Real credit growth decelerated from 2.6%oya to 2.3% in November, with diverging trends for credit to companies and to households (Figure 4). Credit standards continue to improve, with interest rates at levels not seen since 3Q22, a move that is pushed by spreads, but was also noticed on lower funding rates at the margin. Both average non-performing loans (NPL) and arrears decreased a bit, while household indebtedness continued to improve, falling one-tenth and reaching the lowest level since 3Q21.

Figure 4: Outstanding credit growth
![](images/f3fdb94849d9b907f0091c30434f448e86d81f7567a04bb82e4e5bbc40eeb01d.jpg)

## Wider CAD vs stronger FDI

November<sup>’</sup>s current account deficit (CAD) of US\$1.55 billion does not jeopardize our recently updated forecasts for a deficit close to 1.4% of GDP at risk. Foreign direct investment (FDI) inflows reached US\$7.8 billion, almost doubling the consensus estimate and recording the best result for the month since 2018. With that, FDI accelerated three-tenths to 3.0% of GDP, marking at least a pause in the downward trend seen since June (Figure 5).

Figure 5: CAD and FDI US\$ billions, 12-month sum
![](images/03ca1ee9957038891737a6c0a49d7b237f8da636048c12494c300a3acdb6c1fb.jpg)

## Argentina

• December CPI is forecasted at 30%m/m, driving the over-year-ago print to 222%

• Dec-24 CPI unchanged at 210%oya while waiting for the realignment of relative prices

The new administration faces a heavy external burden: a large commercial debt stock, negative FX reserves, PBoC debt and IMF fallen agreement

Getting off on the right foot, the central bank accumulates reserves and the FX gap has narrowed, but pressures linger ahead

We project December CPI at 30%m/m, driving the over-yearago CPI print to 222%oya. The monthly inflation print would be the highest since March 1990, with over-year-ago inflation at levels similar to those reported at the beginning of the Convertibility Plan. Note December would leave a high statistical carryover for January, of about 10.5%-pts in our estimates.

Core inflation would print at 32.9%m/m in December, skyrocketing from the 13.4%m/m reported in November (INDEC). Meanwhile, our preferred metric of underlying inflation (adjusting core inflation by excluding food prices) is also expected to accelerate to 30.9%m/m, coming on the heels of a 12.4% monthly increase reported the previous month. Core goods would have accelerated by 36.7%m/m, but core services “only<sup>”</sup> by 21.4%m/m, which may imply that services prices could increase further in the coming months.

Food inflation is expected to have skyrocketed by 34%m/m in December. The increase was likely broad-based, with above 20%m/m increases in every category, driven by the FX devaluation compounded by the expiration of price-freezing agreements.

Seasonal prices are expected to have increased at 28.9%m/m. On the other side, regulated prices are likely to print well below the general level, at 9.2%m/m, amid energy tariffs freezing and despite the fuel price increase implemented on the month (around 60%m/m). Regulated prices will probably start increasing faster in the coming months given the need to realign relative prices. Indeed, fuels have already increased by another 27% in January, and increases in prepaid medical services, public transport and communication services are also expected on the month. Finally, the government will hold the public hearing to define energy tariff increases this month.

We update our Dec-23 CPI to 222%oya, and keep Dec-24 CPI unchanged at 210%oya while waiting to see how the realignment of relative prices develops. Inflation is expected to remain very elevated in 1H24 amid the need to correct the sizeable relative prices distortion promptly, a necessary condition for stabilization. In our base case of a successful program, inflation is expected to print at around 16%m/m on average during 1H24, decelerating to a monthly average of 4% in the second half of the year (Figure 1). This path would be consistent with December 2024 inflation at 210%oya (330%oya average inflation). Admittedly, the risks to our 2024 forecast are skewed to the upside given the sizable imbalances in place and implementation risks on the stabilization program.

Figure 1: Headline CPI
![](images/6c73410e16cc9ccb5c78d611109cb1cadcb82565726fface66ec62332e3c3cdf.jpg)

## The external burden

The central bank reported the cash-basis current account report for November, the last one under the previous administration. The cash-basis current account accumulated a US\$6.5bn deficit YTD (through November), a strong reversal from the US\$2.4bn surplus reported in the same period last year. As flagged in prior notes, the strong deficit widening has been driven by the merchandise surplus fall. Indeed, the cash-basis merchandise trade balance printed a US\$9.1bn surplus YTD, down from the US\$18.7bn surplus in the same period of 2022. The drop was explained in part by the drought affecting crop production, but exacerbated by poorly designed policy incentives that prompted agents to over-invoice imports and hoard crops amid FX gap widening. On top of that, there were higher interest payments in 2023 amounting to US\$9.1bn YTD, which compares to US\$6.5bn in 2022.

The heavy external inheritance left by the previous administration is reflected in the commercial debt stock accumulated over the last two years. The cash- versus accrued-basis trade balance gap widened against the backdrop of an ongoing sizeable increase in commercial debt as the previous administration limited imports payments amid the scarce FX liquidity. Data through November showed that delayed payments have increased US\$17.9bn YTD, with the commercial debt stock growing by US\$26.7bn since Dec-21 (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Non-energy imports commercial debt
![](images/3d8cb6555e5c919f1f3b488e795deed0c595599480d9b813b45e9ce0b0ad42a4.jpg)
Source: BCRA and J.P. Morgan

Other heavy loads are negative FX reserves, PBoC debt, and the IMF. The new administration inherited a central bank with a very eroded FX balance sheet, as evidenced by negative net reserves at -US\$11bn and USD liquid reserves of only US\$1bn. On the FX liabilities side, the previous administration tapped US\$5bn from the PBoC swap to pay imports, and meet debt service to bondholders and the IMF. Although there is no public information on the contractual repayment terms, unless there is a mechanism to roll over this debt, such usage means fewer dollars will be available for the BCRA to rebuild FX reserves ahead.

Another heavy load left by the outgoing administration is the, de facto fallen, agreement with the IMF. In our view, the Milei administration is likely to fully renegotiate the program. Of note, December capital amortization with the IMF for US\$0.9bn was met thanks to a bridge-loan provided by the CAF (which should be repaid this month), but upcoming maturities for another US\$1.9bn in January require a steadfast negotiation. In our view, administrative delays should not be a deterrent to unlock a US\$3bn disbursement pending from the delayed November 23 review.

## A good start

Starting off on the right foot, the central bank is accumulating reserves and the FX gap is narrowing. Following the initial policy changes, the BCRA has bought reserves of US\$3bn since December 10 (daily average of US\$200mn). In our estimates, net FX reserves stand now at -US\$8.9bn, with USD liquid reserves around US\$3.5bn. Moreover, the FX gap has initially narrowed to around 20%, yet widened to around 30% this week.

Regarding the pace of reserves accumulation, the fact that the new import system only allows payment to start at 25% of the total amount imported one month later is helping the BCRA to accumulate reserves, against further accumulation of commercial debt. Once imports payments start to normalize, the pace at which the BCRA accumulates reserves is likely to slow, at least until the favorable 2Q export seasonality.

On the FX gap, the narrowing is supported by exporters<sup>’</sup> supply of the financial FX (20% of total exports sales), the seasonal end of year increase in the real demand for pesos, and the bulk of capital controls remaining in place. But going forward, the real overvaluation of the official FX (if the crawling peg is maintained at a 2% monthly pace) compounded by deep negative real rates are expected to put further pressure on the FX gap.

Finally, the BCRA has unveiled its strategy to deal with the commercial debt stock. Hard-currency paper from the central bank (BOPREAL), underwritable in pesos, is being offered to those importers that have accumulated commercial debt up to December 11 (for more details please refer to this link). Before year-end importers bought only US\$68mn of the US\$750mn offered. Increased demand should be expected going forward.

The author wishes to thank Juan Goldin, of the Latin America Economics Research team, J.P.Morgan Chase Bank Sucursal Buenos Aires, for his contribution to this report.

## Andeans

• Chile: Sustained growth in ex. mining activity aligns with our baseline for improved momentum

2023 annual GDP growth is expected at 0.04%y/y; we expect 2024 real GDP to gain 2.0%y/y

• Colombia: The government increased the minimum wage by 12.07%

• The real gain of the minimum wage would be similar to that observed last year

Dec-24 inflation is revised higher to 5.2%oya; we wait for Dec-23 CPI to reassess our monetary policy call

## Chile: Growth in ex. mining activity

Headline activity recorded a 0.3%m/m, sa, gain in November, after staying flat in October (revised higher from -0.1%, previously), which owed to the positive evolution of both mining and ex. mining activity.

Ex. mining activity, the most relevant cyclical indicator, printed +0.2%m/m, sa. Following revisions, this proved the fourth consecutive monthly gain, in line with our baseline for improved momentum in the last months of the year (Figure 1). A more granular analysis shows that services recorded +0.3%m/m and other goods +0.9%. On the other side, commerce contracted by 0.6%.

Figure 1:Chile economic activit
![](images/f06fbf4a91ea0f1d5458d2b74a3abce66598c4c0e191c6fa4c7f6e3940c8dfc6.jpg)

Finally, the perennially choppy mining production printed +1.3% on the month, and contributed 0.2%-pt to headline activity.

When the last three-month sequential performance is analyzed, upon revisions, headline activity is running at +2.2%3m/3m, saar, accelerating from 3Q<sup>’</sup>s 1.6%. Ex.mining activity has recovered by 1.0% since August, and runs at 2.4%3m/3m, saar, while mining runs at +0.9%.

In summary, ex.mining activity, the relevant dimension when pondering monetary policy, continues to recover. YTD ex.

mining has gained 1.4%sa (+1.6% annualized), below its potential. Yet, the sequential pace by November printed a bit above the potential threshold. This goes in line with our base scenario for a slightly negative output gap ahead (defined for ex. mining activity).

In terms of forecasts, we project 4Q23 GDP growth at +2.1%ar (+1.1%Q4/Q4), consistent with annual GDP recording 0.04%y/y. For next year, we project real GDP to gain 2.0%y/y, which aligns with+2.4%Q4/Q4.

## Colombia: Impacts of the minimum wage

After weeks of unfruitful negotiations, the government decided to increase the minimum wage (MW), the salary received by more than 2.5 million people in the country, by 12.07%. Although the labor unions initially requested an increase of 18%, they later lowered their aspirations to 12%. When deflating by Dec-24 expected inflation, the real gain of the minimum wage would be similar to that observed last year (Figure 2). Of note, the Colombian economy is characterized by the high frequency of the minimum wage adjustments (once per year) and the relevance of the minimum wage when compared to the median wage, with the MW representing about 90% of the median wage.

Figure 2: Colombia minimum wage real variations
![](images/ca63fd5c4d59eca580b51e84faa86c71fda51e927552992620eb454ff906d5e0.jpg)
Source: MinTrabajo, DANE and J.P. Morgan

Of note, the increase in the minimum wage not only has direct effects on workers' salaries but also serves to define the increase in many services and rates of different sectors. Among the services and rates that will be affected by the increase in salaries are the moderating quotas of the EPS (Health Promoting Entities), medical insurance, and pension contributions. It could even affect the costs of veterinary services. The rise is also related to charges of the Mandatory Traffic Accident Insurance (Soat), the services of the ICA (Colombian Agricultural Institute), the rates of some traffic fines, and notarial rights. That said, Minister Ramírez announced that 88 goods and products will be de-indexed from the minimum wage, in addition to the 116 that were already removed last year.

The impact of the minimum wage on the next 12-month inflation is estimated in the 0.10%-0.14% range. The overall impact depends on the phase of the business cycle, which currently sits at a crossroads. First of all, the output gap has been narrowing and is poised to move into negative territory in early 2024. Yet, despite the economic deceleration, the labor market has proved resilient, particularly the unemployment rate. When adjusting for seasonality, in November the national and urban unemployment rates converged to 10.2% and 9.9%, respectively. Current levels sit well below what the BanRep staff considers to be NAIRU (13%). Yet, the economic deceleration has started to filter into the labor market, as employment has declined on a sequential basis in the last three months through November. Ahead, we expect the unemployment rate to trend higher, though to levels still below the NAIRU threshold. Thus, we are assuming the transmission of MW to inflation will sit closer to the low end of the aforementioned range.

When it comes to our own inflation projection for 2024, we revise Dec <sup>’</sup>24 higher to 5.2%. We had expected the MW to be set below 11% (10.6% was our point estimate). Therefore, the government decree for 12.07% adds about 16bp to the annual forecast. Of note, we have forecasted food prices on the assumption of a strong El Niño event, which so far has had a muted impact on wholesale fresh food. Thus, the projection for ex.food CPI stands at 5.3%oya in December.

Finally, on monetary policy, the government resolution for 12.07% increase in the MW appears as a deterrent for accelerating the easing pace by the end of this month. Yet, we wait for the December CPI print to reassess on our monetary policy call.

The author wishes to thank Juan Goldin, of the Latin America Economics Research team, J.P.Morgan Chase Bank Sucursal Buenos Aires, for his contribution to this report.

## United Kingdom

• Mortgage approvals increase but remain low

• Another downward inflation surprise...

• ...increases pressure on MPC to drop tightening bias

As we begin the new year we set out our broad views for 2024. Growth has stalled last year and the labour market has loosened. A moderation in inflation pressures then prompted the BoE to shift to a high for long strategy after taking rates to 5.25% in order to deal with inflation. The debate has now shifted to when the BoE will cut. We think this is unlikely in 1H24, absent a recession which looks increasingly unlikely, due to persistent wage and services price inflation. While the labor market has loosened and recent nominal outturns have come in soft, evidence of structural damage and low potential growth are likely to leave domestic inflation running too high.

We look for growth to briefly accelerate into 2024 as falling inflation causes consumers to regain some confidence and spend a greater fraction of gains in their real incomes. This could slow the pace of labour market loosening, keep inflation high and further limit the scope for cuts. But we expect this boost to real incomes to fade as the year progresses, and the lagged impact of past tightening to catch up. We assume this will trigger a behavioral response from businesses and households later in the year that results in at least a mild recession. This would likely push the BoE in to easing, and we look for 50bps of cuts in 4Q24. The risk is that easing happens earlier as other central banks consider cuts and inflation moderates more quickly. But pay outturns and expectations in early 2024 probably need to fall significantly below 5% for the BoE to consider an easing as early as spring. For now, we assume the bulk of monetary policy normalization occurs in late 2024 and 2025.

We expect further fiscal easing in 1H24 ahead of a probable election in 2H24, but with the overall fiscal stance close to neutral or tightening slightly compared to this year. The election result will matter more for the macro outlook in 2025, but we do not expect big changes in the overall fiscal stance initially.

## This week’s data

In a fairly quiet week for data, mortgage approvals for new home purchase in November increased to 50.1 thousand (Figure 1). This compares to an average of close to 47k for most of 2023. Approvals remain low but have recovered slightly from the lows of 40k seen at the start of last year. Approvals are likely to remain subdued with rates remaining high. A potential deterioration in the labor market represents the clearest downside risk.

Figure 1: BoE mortgage approvals vs. secured net lending
![](images/a2761097ba16d2234019a76c1fbdd4d490ce87f9da29463b2048b8dcdaffc331.jpg)
The BoE<sup>’</sup>s DMP survey of businesses showed inflation expectations for December at 5.4%oya. Year ahead expectations for the CPI were at 4.0%oya, and 3 year ahead expectations at 3.9%oya.

Figure 2: DMP business price expectations
![](images/3d65656f9bbb106fd7a28e463b40cb64085e437ca8f89052fb1c54440f1f1630.jpg)

Figure 3: CPI inflation
%3m/3m, saar, dotted line denotes J.P. Morgan forecast
![](images/60a5eed3d6541dfed395bec8ffbb0c9c09052b7b371059dfcc61f7f432dff9bb.jpg)
Source: ONS, J.P. Morgan

## November inflation downward surprise

Headline CPI fell 0.2%m/m in November based on our seasonal adjustment, taking the oya rate down from 4.6% to 3.9% (JPMorgan 4.5%; Consensus 4.4%; BoE 4.6%). Core prices were flat on the month, sa (nsa: -0.3%m/m), leaving the oya rate 0.6%-pt lower at 5.1% (Figure 3). This was wellbelow expectations (JPMorgan 5.7%; Consensus 5.6%). The BoE is focusing on services inflation at present, and the annu al rate there fell more than expected from 6.6% to 6.3% (JPMorgan 6.5%; BoE: 6.9%). RPI inflation was down from 6.1% to 5.3%oya (JPMorgan 5.8%; Consensus 5.7%).

The details showed the softness in the core inflation print came mainly from core goods, which dropped 0.6%m/m, sa (Figure 4). Core services were up two tenths on the month (sa), however, this was also a weaker outcome than in the past couple of months (and relative to our forecast). Within core goods, several categories fell on the month, with particularly large declines posted in vehicles and other recreational goods. The former has now seen prices falling for six consecutive months.

Within core services, rents, other housing services, and transport insurance were strong on the month, but around half of the categories were flat or declined. The biggest fall was in transport services likely reflecting spillover effects from lower petrol prices. Falling petrol prices also pulled down headline inflation. Other headline components were mixed with food and non-alcoholic beverages down 0.3%m/m, sa, and alcohol & tobacco up 1.0%m/m, sa.

Figure 4: Core inflation
%m/m, sa by J.P. Morgan, excludes transport services
![](images/7422b1c8fb55e772ef59b2c4074bebc6292046ca76334cb4c3b15a898adfea5b.jpg)

Overall, the November report was soft on most accounts. While goods inflation was the main reason for the weaker outcome, services inflation was also clearly below projections. The BoE has downplayed recent low services inflation prints, but with the gap to the bank<sup>’</sup>s forecast now widening from 0.3%-pt to 0.6%-pt it should be harder to ignore. Data on wages and labor market tightness have also come out soft lately. The risks are therefore shifting. The BoE will need to show at least some incremental response if recent downside surprises on the nominal side continue. A mild change would be to drop its tightening bias, but maintain the extended period language. That is possible in February.

That being said, we do not think the MPC is close to cutting, and we have maintained our call that rates will not be cut until 4Q24. On a 3m/3m annualized basis, services inflation is still running at 4.5%. While this is a large downshift from the 10.2% peak in June, but we do not expect it to continue slowing so rapidly. Also, there is little in the growth data arguing for a dovish BoE pivot at present (with e.g. the December PMI positing a solid gain).

## Emerging Europe

• Poland: Large non-core downside surprise to Dec CPI

• Israel: Why wait? BOI cuts 25bp

• Türkiye: Inflation trend likely to pick up in 1Q24

• Czech Republic: Strong 2H23 rescues budget

According to the preliminary release, Polish consumer prices were up just 0.1%m/m in December, leading headline inflation to slow to 6.1%oya, from 6.6%, considerably lower than expected by markets (Consensus: 6.5%oya, JPM: 6.8%).

Looking in the scarce details, we see that food and energy prices drove the downside surprise. After briefly accelerating in November, food price inflation was muted again in December, with prices advancing only 0.2%m/m (we expected 0.8%m/m), leading to a drop in annual food CPI to 5.9%oya, from 7.3%. Given that food weighs 27% in the basket, this accounts for most of the decline in CPI. Adjusted for seasonality, food prices fell 0.15%m/m, and are trending at 2-3% annualized pace. Fuel prices developed more or less as expected, dropping 1.8%m/m, and on over-year-ago terms are now at -6%oya.

Core CPI is not published with the flash release, but, triangulating the available data, we estimate that core prices were up 0.35%m/m in December, fully in line with our forecast. If confirmed, this implies core CPI falling to 7%oya in October, from 7.3%. Sequentially, core CPI is up a bit from November, and is running at an annualized pace of around 3.9%.

We expect inflation will fall abruptly in early 2024, mainly on favorable energy base effects, as last year<sup>’</sup>s massive energy tariff adjustments are followed by frozen prices this year (until mid-year at least), resulting in the “other energy<sup>”</sup> category dropping from 9.8%oya to -2.3% in January. Food inflation, given the very low sequential momentum also still has plenty of room to decline, and we see it at 3% by March. As a result, overall CPI will also fall close to the 3% mark in March. However, we are more skeptical about core inflation declining as much, in an environment of double-digit wage growth, fiscal generosity and demand recovery. We expect core CPI to oscillate between 4-4.5% in most of 2024, which, together with the normalization of food VAT in 2Q24 and some energy tariff adjustment in 2H24, should push CPI back to the 4-5% range in the remainder of the year (Figure 1).

The NBP is more likely to use the last couple of downside surprises as an argument to justify it did well to cut in September/October, and less so to justify a new dovish impetus. The focus instead will be on the sticky core inflation, and how it it still running clearly above target. Absent major

downside surprises to core inflation in the next few months, the March projection of the NBP research department is likely to show a protracted overshoot of core CPI, returning to the band in 2025 only, which the MPC will use to justify staying on hold. Still, with core CPI in the low 4s in late 2024, we see some room for the NBP to cautiously deliver two 25bp cuts in 4Q24.

## BOI: 25bp cut delivered

The BoI cut the policy rate 25bp to 4.0% at this week<sup>’</sup>s meeting, delivering on expectations that lower realized inflation and a stronger shekel will prevail over other concerns of the MPC. To be sure, the list of BoI concerns remains vast — from uncertainties about the scale and duration of war and its economic impact, to the direction of fiscal policy and elevated ILS volatility. But, as we anticipated, downward inflation surprises, lessening concerns about financial stability domestically, and more supportive external financial conditions were enough for the MPC to act already at the January meeting.

BoI<sup>’</sup>s economic assessments and BoI Research forecasts have not changed much. The statement noted a significant decline in inflation momentum, the recent recovery of economic activity and the stability in markets. The governor spent even more time (than previously) relaying BoI concerns about fiscal policy, which is now seen as one of key risks for the longterm outlook. The BoI assessed that even after the war ends there will be a permanent increase in defence and other spending of around 1.5%-pt of GDP, which, if not addressed, could put government debt on an unsustainable trajectory. The Research forecast continued to indicate that the policy rate would be 3.75-4.0% a year from now, while governor Yaron mentioned that this forecast was appropriate.

With little new input from the BoI we keep our rate forecasts unchanged. We expect another 25bp cut in February, with the policy rate reaching 3.75% by end-24. We continue to expect that relatively loose fiscal policy will be a constraint for a more aggressive policy easing by the BoI.

## Türkiye: Dec CPI in line with expectations

Headline CPI inflation came in at 2.9% m/m in December, close to the market consensus of 3% (JPMe: 2.7%). CPI inflation increased to 64.8%oya, from 62% in November, mainly driven by food and energy prices. Natural gas use above the freely-provided 25 cubic meters pushed December inflation up by 0.3%pt. Food prices rose 4.8% m/m, pushing up annual food inflation to 72% oya in December, from 67%. Services prices rose 2.4% m/m in December, pushing up annual services inflation to a new all-time record level at 90.7%, from 89.7%oya in November.

We see the over-year-ago disinflation process in 2H24 as more challenging as services inflation will remain sticky. Core goods prices rose 2.2% m/m in November, pushing up over-year-ago inflation to 52.8% in December, from 52.2%. As a result, core inflation rose 2.3% m/m, leading oya core inflation to 70.6% in December, from 69.9% in November.

Core inflation momentum (3m change, saar, %) continued to fall thanks to services prices, but core goods inflation momentum was marginally up in December. We expect significant acceleration in the core inflation trend in 1Q24, due to the higher-than-expected minimum wage hike of 49% (JPMe: 40%), and second-round effects from administered price hikes and tax adjustments indexed to realized inflation.

Headline over-year-ago inflation is set to rise until May 2024 (Figure 2) owing to higher-than-expected wage hikes and unfavourable base effects driven by natural gas prices. The authorities prioritized fiscal discipline over disinflation in January as they preferred using the revaluation rate of 58% for administered price hikes (highway, bridge fees, etc) directly, and did not reduce automatic tax adjustments in tobacco and motor fuel prices indexed to realized inflation. We revise up our year-end inflation forecast to 42%oya (previously 40%). We will monitor the extent of real appreciation of lira and how the economic slowdown will offset the effects of realized wage hikes coupled with expected electricity and natural gas price hikes after the March municipal elections.

Recent data flow reinforces our view that the CBRT will keep monetary policy tight for longer. We continue to expect the CBRT to hike its policy rate by 250bp to 45% this month, and end the tightening cycle. That said, we do not rule out further tightening beyond January by the CBRT depending on inflation prints in 1Q24. We believe that the CBRT will keep the door open for further rate hikes in its forward guidance at the January MPC meeting. We continue to expect the CBRT to keep its policy rate at 45% (or more) this year to reign in inflation.

## Czech Republic: 2023 budget within target

The Czech state budget ended 2023 within the Government<sup>’</sup>s target, after a remarkable shift in execution performance during the second half of the year, which made up for a poor start. In December, the budget posted a deficit of CZK19.4 billion , which leads to a full-year budget result of -CZK288.5 billion (Figure 3), marginally better than the Government<sup>’</sup>s CZK295 billion target. Recall that by May, the deficit was already a cumulated CZK271 billion, on track to be the worse in decades, but in the subsequent period June-December the budget was nearly balanced (small deficit of CZK17 billion). We estimate the state budget deficit was 4% of GDP in 2023, compared to 5.3% in 2022 and 6.9% in 2021.

Figure 3: Czech state budget execution - cumulative (cash basis) % of GDP
![](images/879f625a6ad4ef1564e6c7628c247d0802a01189680de3fc314664130db9e494.jpg)

The roller-coaster ride of 2023 resulted from a time mismatch between on the one hand, energy subsidies which kicked off from the start of the year, and, on the other hand, bumpy revenue flows, like the windfall tax (September and December), and dividends from CEZ (August) which took place only in the second half of the year. These together brought in CZK111.6 billion in revenue, or 1.3% of GDP. It is remarkable also that in a year where such extraordinary revenues were obtained from companies, corporate tax was still up 0.5%-pts of GDP. Less surprising perhaps, consumer taxes (VAT and excises) fell 0.3%-pts of GDP vs 2022,due to the weakness in the consumer.

Hungary:

On hold: 5.75%. See main text for details.

On the expenditure side, the big change of 2023 was the CZK76.1 billion in energy subsidies (1% of GDP), which despite being sizeable were less than had been budgeted (CZK100bn) and mostly covered by the windfall tax and revenue cap. All in all, energy measures on the revenue and spending side had a net cost of CZK18.5 billion, equivalent to just 0.25% of GDP.

The Czech consolidated Government budget balance is typically better than the state budget (Central) due to the usual surpluses at the Local Government level. Given that local Government has been delivering a budgetary surplus (CZK77.3 billion until September), 2023 should be no exception and hence the ESA budget balance will likely be sub 4% of GDP, which was our estimate so far. The impact of social security funds is likely to be small. We reduce our estimate of the 2023 budget deficit to 3.6% of GDP (from 4%), to be followed next year by a reduction to 2.4% of GDP.

## South Africa

• November fiscal receipts reached 59.1% of target, close to the historical average

• Provisional cashflow data for lumpy December receipts also broadly on track

This lessens revenue concerns with slippage limited to 0.2%-0.4%pts of GDP

High-frequency fiscal data and preliminary changes in cash balances suggest relatively contained fiscal slippage after recent MTBPS revisions. Revenue momentum in the three months to November improved somewhat to 5.7%oya, after a contraction at mid-year. This brings cumulative receipts since begin of the fiscal year in April to 59.1% of target, now only marginally short of the historical average.

At the same time, spending growth, after adjusting for SOE support, has slowed in October and November to 4.7%, after rising at a pace of 9.6%oya in the six months to September. Achieving the lowered full-year expenditure target will require tight execution in 1Q24 and may still be challenging to reach. Yet, the extent of overspending should likely be limited to 0.3%pts of GDP or less.

A key concern was the performance of mining tax receipts, due in June and December. While full details of December receipts are only available at the end of this month, some inference can be drawn based on Treasury issuance and change in cash balances, as was released this week. We infer a December fiscal surplus of R33.7bn, from R45bn a year earlier, which we think is in line or even marginally better than expected. While spending restraint last month may have helped dampen the decline in surplus, it is likely that revenue also held up and consequently should be within R10bn of the full-year target. That said, given that the month-end fell on a weekend, some sizeable adjustments to preliminary data often take place. We continue to look for relatively limited slippage of 0.2%-0.4%pts of GDP compared to the revised MTBPS projections for a 4.7% deficit excluding the Eskom bailout (JPMe: 5.1%; 6.2% including Eskom bailout).

## Australia and New Zealand

• Australia<sup>’</sup>s monthly CPI indicator is expected to increase 0.3%m/m (nsa) in November

However base-effects mean the annual rate will slow from 4.9%oya to 4.3%oya

• Retail sales are forecast to benefit from Black Friday related sales and discounting

It was a quiet first week of 2024 with no domestic data of note. The coming week is likely to be more eventful with the monthly CPI indicator for November released on January 10. As a refresher, the October number came in meaningfully below expectations (actual: 4.9%oya, consensus: 5.2%oya) with government assistance and subsidy programs contributing to the downside surprise. This distortion was most pronounced for residential rents which posted a rare decline (-0.4%m/m) following changes to the Commonwealth Rental Assistance (CRA) program. The unadjusted number was closer to+0.7%m/m, broadly in line with the recent trend and consistent with our view that supply/demand in the rental market remains problematic. The annual rate of electricity price inflation also slowed (Figure 1), though the effect was somewhat muted given new rebates on the East Coast were counterbalanced by the lapsing of subsidies in other parts of the country. Importantly, we expect the effect from all subsidy/ rebate payments will be less pronoucned in November and contribute to a recovery in the monthly CPI indicator.

Figure 1: Housing related inflation
![](images/4e043ba21a809e85dac96ff67c35ec8f7e6dc1ab4e7ad8b80654e051ebe5d477.jpg)

Beyond housing, we also anticipate modest price increases in the holiday travel/accommodation, insurance and household furnishing subgroups following softer than forecast outcomes the month prior. Tracking data suggest fuel prices declined in November, while food prices are also expected to tick lower. In aggregate we look for November<sup>’</sup>s CPI indicator to increase 0.3%m/m (nsa), however base-effects mean the annual rate is still likely to slow from 4.9%oya to 4.3%oya.

## A temporary lift in retail spending

Next week<sup>’</sup>s retail sales report for November will provide an important health check on consumer spending in late 2024. In recent years discounting activity in November has inflated spending at the expense of the adjacent months, with October<sup>’</sup>s weaker than forecast outcome owing, in part, to consumers delaying consumption to take account of Black Friday sales. This dynamic has been evident in recent years (Figure 2, highlighted in orange circle), with retail sales vaulting 1.5%m/m in November 2022 before falling 4.2%m/m in December.

Figure 2: Monthly retail sales
![](images/c9b4c6cc7a2ea1249c7744f43921706ee5835f41d68d2ed210db39a181b1ebb2.jpg)

It<sup>’</sup>s difficult to quantify magnitudes given lingering COVID effects and the influence of the ABS<sup>’</sup>s seasonal adjustment process, but anecdotal reports suggest retail spending is likely to have accelerated in November. Accordingly, we expect the coming week<sup>’</sup>s retail report to show sales increased 0.8%m/ m, but also note that this strength is likely to be unwound, at least partially, in subsequent prints. If realized, this outcome would see the over year ago rate slow to 0.5% the slowest pace of retail spending since lockdown induced weakness in 2021 (Figure 3).

Figure 3: Australian retail sales
![](images/9808d5efcc8ada2e60186c932335dd422043342db8738d6eb6a76ac14db8f27f.jpg)

## Housing keeps climbing

Australia<sup>’</sup>s Corelogic house price data reported that national dwelling prices increased 8%oya in December 2023 (Figure 4). The national aggregate masks some interesting divergence across the various states/capital cities with prices in Brisbane and Adelaide surging \~30%, while price outcomes in the South-Eastern states were less impressive. Indeed, house prices in Sydney rose 11%oya, whereas Melbourne saw appreciation in the low single digits (3%oya).

Figure 4: Australian dwelling prices
![](images/109058ceb157cd3ba896c0492911670520b40e5fc2347639640806e990c9f1f0.jpg)

While fixed rate mortgage activity spiked during COVID, these loans were relatively short term in nature (on average less than three years) with roughly 80% of current mortgage holders still on variable rate loans. The structure of the mortgage market means the transmission from RBA rate hikes to household spending occurs quickly relative to economies where the mortgage market is dominated by fixed rate loans, for example New Zealand and the US. Our forecast anticipates that the cash rate has now peaked so most of the adjustment in household debt servicing costs has now occurred, and we forecast the cash rate to hold current levels through 2024. In our view cash rate stability is consistent with modest single digit house price growth as weak dwelling construction growth and still strong net migration bias prices higher.

## Greater China

• China: NBS and Caixin manufacturing PMIs diverged again in December; policy support in progress

• Hong Kong: Retail sales beat expectations

• Taiwan: Manufacturing PMI weakened with significant easing in export orders

• CPI inflation came off modestly to 2.7%oya, core CPI remained somewhat sticky

• Next week: China FX reserves, inflation, trade and credit, Taiwan trade

The Caixin and NBS PMIs have moved in opposite directions in both November and December, sending confusing signals about the economy<sup>’</sup>s near-term growth momentum. For the month of December, the NBS manufacturing PMI disappointed again, falling 0.4-pt to 49.0 (the lowest reading in seven months), while the Caixin manufacturing PMI delivered a further upside surprise, rising 0.1-pt to 50.8 (Figure 1). After China<sup>’</sup>s reopening, the NBS manufacturing PMI outperformed the Caixin reading only in 1Q . Since 2Q, the NBS manufacturing PMI has generally underperformed the exportoriented Caixin reading, highlighting concerns about lingering softness in domestic-demand conditions, while manufacturers seem to have intensified competition in the export market, including via price cuts. In particular, it is interesting to note that the export orders component of the Caixin manufacturing PMI rose 0.5-pt to 49.5 in December, the highest reading in six months.

Figure 1: China manufacturing PMIs
![](images/77aa9e219dff968e5decefb26caff41f08f360fa0f8f0b53e6ff8fb357a2e56b.jpg)

While the NBS non-manufacturing PMI rose modestly by 0.2-pt to 50.4 in December, it still registered the second lowest reading since the beginning of the year (Figure 2). Service activity remained soft with further slowing in travel-related activities amid cold weather. The construction activity index rose further by 1.9-pt to 56.9 (the highest reading in seven months), suggesting solid infra-FAI related activity amid fiscal policy support. Taking the manufacturing and non-manufacturing sectors together, the NBS composite PMI output

inched down by 0.1-pt to 50.3 in December.

Figure 2: China NBS non-manufacturing PM
![](images/cdcfecb29bf9d33fc4bbed6edf56d0fff81e3c733333d207821928007f06e6c4.jpg)

On the policy front, there is lingering deflation pressure in the economy, concern of ongoing weakness in domestic demand, and the recent moderation in external headwinds (easing global core yields and lower USD/CNY). Hence we have brought forward the forecast for the PBOC<sup>’</sup>s next policy rate cut (10bp) to 1Q (likely in March), and have added another 10bp rate cut in 3Q (likely in September) in our baseline forecast. We continue to look for two 25bp RRR cuts this year, with more active use of structural monetary policy instruments as quasi-fiscal measures to supplement fiscal operations. Indeed, the PBOC injected liquidity of 350 billion yuan via the Pledged Supplementary Lending (PSL) facility to policy banks in December, which likely goes to support public housing construction and urban village development, a policy development in line with our baseline assumptions.

## Hong Kong: Solid retail sales

Hong Kong<sup>’</sup>s retail sales volume came in above expectations in November, rising 12.4%oya (Figure 3). Seasonally adjusted, we estimate that retail sales volume rose 2.4%m/m sa in November, offsetting the 2.2% decline in October. Clothing, department store and jewelry sales led the growth, rising 11.0%m/m sa, 8.1% and 7.8% respectively. Meanwhile, retail sales volume of consumer durable goods fell 1.0%m/m sa.

Figure 3: Hong Kong retail sales and private consumption
![](images/f264626552f070297f7a1dc4a86a18af8db94e54428bd3fd62b85eb473974ca0.jpg)

Looking ahead, we believe inbound tourism will still be the driving factor of consumption growth in HK, yet the imbalance between inbound and outbound visitor recovery may continue to weigh on HK<sup>’</sup>s consumption. Meanwhile, the low unemployment environment, household income growth, together with various promotional campaigns and activities launched by the government, should also continue to benefit the retail sector.

## Taiwan: Manufacturing PMI weakened

Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s manufacturing PMI seems to have lost momentum going into the year end, with a decline of 1.2-pt in December to reach 47.1. It reversed the four consecutive monthly gains through November. The output component fell 1.8-pt to 45.2 (Figure 4) and the export orders component dropped 3.5-pt to 44.9, hinting at a near-term easing in industrial activity and exports. In addition, though the manufacturing PMI finished goods inventory component fell 1.5-pt, the new orders component fell more, by 2.2-pt. It is thus concerning that the manufacturing PMI order to inventory ratio fell further to 0.97 in December (vs. 0.99 in November), the lowest reading in four months.

Figure 4: Taiwan manufacturing PMI output and IP
![](images/e3059b2f831e652ea6e316697253b65f95e63de09b5d8867504a0a98c62e660f.jpg)

We have highlighted some cautious signs for near-term tech sector activity, including softer-than-expected November export orders, as well as a notable decline in Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s imports of raw materials and intermediate goods in November (down 12.5% m/ sa). Another concern is that the underlying trend in Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s capital goods imports remained on the soft side (down 27.6% 3m/3m saar in November).

## CPI inflation came off modestly to 2.7%oya

Headline CPI inflation eased modestly to 2.7%oya in December (vs. 2.9%oya in November), rising 0.2% m/m sa (Figure 5). Food prices and fuel costs came in stable in December. Food prices remained flat in sequential terms (0.0% m/m sa, or up 4.7%oya). The aggregate energy-related CPI component also stayed flat (0.0% m/m sa, or up 1.2%oya), reflecting the latest trend in global oil prices. Core CPI inflation remained

somewhat sticky, ticking up to 2.43%oya in December, or rising moderately by 0.2% m/m sa. CPI items related to post-Covid recovery in domestic activity showed moderate increases.

Figure 5: Taiwan headline and core CPI
![](images/82edadf3e7cba29220a17a476d00cf6d1e97b9108d59b7fb6c13953f072416ff.jpg)

Looking ahead, our global commodity team expects the Brent oil price to average at \$83/bbl in 2024, suggesting imported inflation will unlikely be a key concern for Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s inflation outlook. Besides, core CPI inflation may gradually ease going forward, as the most powerful phase of post-COVID domestic demand recovery is behind us, with signs of slowing employment growth in recent months. We expect Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s headline CPI inflation to ease gradually from an average 2.5%yoy in 2023 to 2.1% in 2024, while core CPI inflation will likely ease from 2.6%yoy to 2.0%.

With a moderate growth and inflation outlook, we expect the Taiwan central bank to keep policy rates on hold through 2024. While the Fed will likely cut rates by 125bp in 2024, we doubt the Taiwan central bank will rush to follow suit, not only because Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s rate increase has been rather modest, but also because real interest rates remain below the historical average.

## Review of past week’s data

Taiwan:

## Korea

• Strong November IP was offset by weaker non-manufacturing activities

• Customs exports advanced in December, 4Q marked strong nominal exports growth

• CPI inflation continued a gradual moderation

The manufacturing sector data released during the year-end holiday season was a mixed bag, with strong November IP, monthly growth in customs exports in December, and a fall in the manufacturing PMI. Taking the non-manufacturing activi ties through November together with manufacturing production, risk to our 4Q real GDP growth forecast (2.0%q/q, saar) looks to be balanced. Looking forward for 1Q24, the data do not suggest a material change in growth momentum in the near term, in our view. Meanwhile, CPI inflation in December was broadly in line with our expectation for a gradual moderation. Without major data surprises, we expect that the Bank of Korea will maintain its previous stance to stand pat with hawkish guidance at next week<sup>’</sup>s MPC meeting.

## Strong Nov IP, weaker non-manufacturing

Korea<sup>’</sup>s industrial production rebounded strongly by 3.3%m m, sa in November after falling 3.8% in October. Looking through the monthly volatility, IP<sup>’</sup>s sequential growth remained at a double-digit annualized growth rate (12.3%3m/3m, saar) in November, but we continue to expect that 4Q<sup>’</sup>s IP growth should slow down after 7.9% annualized growth in 3Q given the high base at the end of 3Q. In the details, tech production rebounded strongly as expected after October<sup>’</sup>s fall, likely due to under-controlled seasonality. Excluding tech products, non-tech production rose by a relatively modest 0.8% after falling for two months. As a result, the striking gap between tech vs. non-tech production growth continues, as tech production is growing at 64.2%3m/3m, saar while non-tech production contracted 3.3% (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Manufacturing production %3m/3m, saar, both axes
![](images/58374412a47558d979a568f8a29f432484c30bacc27e63da9c30668c9bb3d222.jpg)

Offsetting the strong IP growth in November, non-manufacturing sector production broadly fell in November. As a

result, aggregating the IP and non-manufacturing activity, the monthly GDP growth proxy index (non-farm all industry output) rose only modestly by 0.5%m/m, sa after a 1.8% fall in October, and we continue to expect that three-month sequential growth of the non-farm all industry output should slow down by end-4Q. We continue to see a balanced risk to our 2.0%q/q, saar real GDP growth outlook for the current quarter.

## Customs exports rose in December

Seasonally adjusted by J.P. Morgan, customs exports rose 1.0%m/m, sa in December after edging down 0.4% in November, and the three-month trend growth accelerated to 25.9%3m/3m, saar. In the details, both tech (+1.7%m/m, sa) and non-tech (+0.8%) exports rose in December, while the main driver of export recovery continues to be semiconductors (Figure 2). Semiconductor exports rose 6.0% for the fifth consecutive month of strong gains, and the tech sector exports<sup>’</sup> three-month trend growth further accelerated strongly to 68.1%3m/3m, saar.

Figure 2: Customs exports - tech vs. nontech US\$ bn, sa
![](images/e76a41ec9db0043d65ac68f4df25357c9c54353b5198735192cd3c6e0de233e2.jpg)

## PMI edged down in December

Korea<sup>’</sup>s manufacturing PMI edged down by 0.1pt to 49.9 in December from November<sup>’</sup>s 1-1/2-year high. In the details, both the output and new orders indices fell marginally in December after marking 19- and 16-month highs a month ago, respectively.

Figure 3: Korea manufacturing output - PMI vs. IP
![](images/2412950897198d9fe4d21678ade63d390e01b2663a8745f902a6fa81b33e9627.jpg)

The output PMI edged down by 0.1pt to 49.8, partially reversing a cumulative 3.4pt rise over the past five months. Its level has recently implied more modest IP growth than actual outturns (Figure 3), possibly because the latest IP strength is concentrated in the tech sector while broader nontech production has remained weak. The new orders index fell by 0.3pt in December after rising a total of 1.5pts in September-November.

## CPI inflation moderated as expected

Korea<sup>’</sup>s CPI rose 3.2%oya in December following 3.8% and 3.3% prints in the past two months, which was broadly in line with expectations for a gradual moderation. If seasonally adjusted, both headline CPI and core CPI (ex. energy and food) rose 0.1%m/m, sa in December to continue modest monthly gains following 0.0% and 0.1% pace (for headline and core, respectively) in November. As a result, the threemonth sequential gain decelerated to 3.6%3m/3m, saar and 2.7% for headline and core prices.

Figure 4: Inflation forecast
![](images/b2053b7f3dfb45f767acaaf81295a30ab12697f130312ccd9177f7dbf5450a80.jpg)
Source: NSO and J.P. Morgan

Looking forward, we expect headline over-year-ago inflation to moderate slowly to hover around the mid-to-upper 2%oya pace by 3Q24, and to approach the 2% target by end-3Q24 (Figure 4). In our updated forecasts, 2024<sup>’</sup>s annual inflation should be 2.5%y/y for the headline and 2.1% for core inflation, which are slightly lower than the Bank of Korea<sup>’</sup>s 2024 forecasts in November (2.6% and 2.3%, respectively).

## ASEAN

• Singapore<sup>’</sup>s November manufacturing output fell, led by tech; expect trend to remain positive

• Air travel continues to improve, but freight has stalled since 2022

• Domestic demand expected to remain soft following the 1 January 1% GST hike

• Core CPI expected to drift lower, but watching the monthly data closely

The incoming data in Singapore continue to convey a mixed picture of the economy, with external-facing services continuing to recover, while goods production took a step down in November, led by tech. The weakness in tech contrasts with the more upbeat data in North Asia, and we expect the underlying trend for tech output to remain resilient. Despite the anticipated resilience in tech, domestic activity, consumption especially, has slowed and is expected to remain lackluster as pent-up demand slows and post-COVID-19 employment gains normalize. This is already evident in the 4Q23 retail sales and food and beverage consumption data.

We expect softer domestic demand to curtail domestic sources of inflationary pressure and restrain the impact of the 1% GST hike on 1 January. That said, we are mindful that there could be idiosyncratic price pressures beyond the GST hike that could emerge and are watching the evolution of core CPI for such developments in the coming months.

Given the expected path of core CPI, we expect the MAS to remain on hold through this year at its quarterly meetings. These are to be held no later than 29 January and 12 April, while the July and October meeting deadlines have yet to be determined.

## November IP pares on tech

Singapore<sup>’</sup>s November manufacturing production came in much weaker than expected, down 7.8%m/m, sa and up 1.0%oya (J.P. Morgan: 12.7%oya and consensus: 2.2%oya; Figure 1). The weaker-than-expected outturn was driven by tech (-13.5%m/m, sa) and non-tech/biomedical output, which tends to be a fairly useful representation of broader manufacturing activity (Figure 2). The sequential decline in November tech output contrasts with the uplift evident in the bellwethers of Korea and Taiwan, which provides some comfort that the underlying trend remains constructive (Figure 3).

Figure 1: Singapore industrial production 2010=100, sa
![](images/8dfa6d6971c20beab629c2a6861cea1c805889652183a7a14bdfdeb9c65f84b9.jpg)

Figure 2: Singapore industrial production 2010=100, sa
![](images/b51d88e947a5780cc99aa5979621acc0376c078e44ea2ef144ed139f339f60f7.jpg)

Figure 3: Asia tech output
![](images/c6a30c5b8c678e8bd81590fcc11d5bffbd0c39dbc6d1c295cec370be8d6e3e5b.jpg)

## Multi-speed domestic demand

Although we remain cautiously positive on the ongoing lift in tech, our view on domestic demand remains cautious. We expect the post-COVID-19 reopening lift to be largely behind us, while the outlook for private consumption will be clouded by external uncertainties and softer labor markets. Thus, we expect marginal consumption to slow. This dynamic is evident in both the air traffic movements of passengers, which remain positive, and the more dour performance in the domestic-facing indicators of retail sales and food & beverage consumption (Figures 4 and 5).

Figure 7: Singapore core CPI %3m/3m, saar

Figure 4: Singapore air passenger and cargo movements 000s passengers, sa
![](images/429c5da1f54d123d4f8c243dd4392b9a916cbd37e4c66bc2d9579e232abca6a9.jpg)

Figure 5: Singapore domestic demand indicators Index, 2017=100, sa
![](images/a7214cb873b61cbef94178fe4f0596c63496b2fe0502131405e8e8d8c2182223.jpg)
Core CPI expected to remain benign

Given the expected path for domestic activity, we also expect underlying inflation to remain on a downtrend in both sequential and over-year-ago terms (Figure 6).

Figure 6: Singapore core inflation
![](images/70564fd5947a6ec192ecb476a09f98c30d70af33b8bcf47eac77528071ec31dc.jpg)
Source: DoS, J.P. Morgan forecasts in shaded area

Singapore<sup>’</sup>s November core CPI (which excludes private road transport and accommodation) rose 0.2%m/m, sa and was up 3.2%oya, in line with expectations (J.P. Morgan: 3.1%oya and consensus: 3.2%oya). In trend sequential terms, core CPI rose 2.9%3m/3m, saar. In the details, goods inflation picked up slightly, while services inflation continues to display gradual easing. Within core services, the downtrend in food prices has somewhat halted. But given the lack of supply-side price pressures, food prices should continue to stabilize going into 2024 (Figures 7 and 8). Notwithstanding the El Niño effect and the lagged impact of higher rental prices, as well as the

1%pt GST hike on 1 January 2024, the trend easing in core CPI should remain in place, in our view.

![](images/fbf75510f12179cfe75ba13385be81962857fce54d8a60fcb8eabd18e348f0f3.jpg)

Figure 8: Singapore food prices
![](images/a370a447cca7e9c54cc0598d661ddc91f503e9e21efdb7c48c2a15f4866c7ef4.jpg)

As noted, services core inflation has been easing, and we expect the combination of slowing domestic demand and the ensuing slowing in employment to help temper domestic price pressures.

In this context, employment growth has slowed and vacancy rates have declined since peaking in 1Q22, suggesting that underlying domestic price pressures should also ease (Figures 5 and 6). That said, although pressures look to be easing, the recent experience suggests that vigilance remains important, given the lags in the inflation process and sector differentiation. To this end, monitoring the underlying details of the incoming inflation data remains important.

## MAS expected to remain on hold

Given the expected forecast path for core CPI, we expect to see no changes to MAS policy this year. The MAS will be moving to a quarterly schedule for its monetary policy statements, which will be released in January, April, July and October, from a biannual schedule, usually in April and October, in the past. The January meeting will be held no later than 29 January and the April meeting no later than the 12th.

## India

• NSO pegs full-year FY24 GDP growth at 7.3%

• Next week, headline CPI to accelerate to 5.9% oya and IP to soften to 3.5%

• But 1Q24 CPI now expected at 4.5-5%, lower than previously envisaged, on softer food prices

Food prices falling despite a patchy monsoon on account of aggressive policy measures

This week NSO projected FY24 GDP growth at 7.3% which implied a strong 7.0% growth for 2HFY24. In our view there are downside risks to NSO<sup>’</sup>s estimates (details below). Next week we expect December CPI to accelerate to 5.9% oya from 5.6% in November, and November IP to fall sharply to 3.5% oya from 11.7% in October. However, both data releases should be taken with pinch of salt as headline inflation is expected to fall sharply from January, and the sharp deceleration in industrial activity is just a reflection of base effect. For 1Q24, CPI inflation is now forecasted to be lower in the 4.5-5% range, below RBI<sup>’</sup>s forecast of 5.2%.

## NSO pegs FY24 GDP growth at 7.3%

The National Statistical Office (NSO) projected FY24 (year ending March 2024) growth at 7.3% oya. This is higher than our forecast (6.5%) and the market consensus (6.7%). With 1HFY24 printing at 7.7% oya, 2HFY24 growth imputed from NSO estimates is a strong 7.0%oya. The NSO annual estimate is based on the extrapolation of strong April to November data. However, we expect activity to slow in 2HFY24. Hence in our view there are downside risks to NSO<sup>’</sup>s growth projection.

For example, NSO expects investment growth to accelerate from 9.5% oya in 1HFY24 to 11.0% oya in 2HFY24, which in our view would be challenging as government capex – which has been the main driver of investment growth – is likely to slow due to budget constraints. More broadly, after front loading of the government spending in 1HFY24, fiscal impulse is likely to turn much tighter in 2HFY24 for the government to hit the budget target. However, CSO expects public administration growth to remain unchanged at 7.7% oya between 1HFY24 and 2HFY24. Furthermore, NSO projects export growth to pick up from -1.7% oya in 1HFY24 to 4.3% oya in 2HFY24 despite expected slowing in the global economy. All in all, there are downside risks to the NSO<sup>’</sup>s advance estimate of 7.3% even as there are upside risks to our FY24 GDP forecast of 6.5%oya. Lastly, the NSO projected nominal GDP growth of 8.9% for FY24 which is lower than the 10.5% growth projected in the budget.

## Dec CPI to accelerate but below 5% in 1Q24

December CPI is expected to accelerate to 5.9% from 5.5% in November, both on account of unfavourable base effects but also because the seasonal decline of food prices has not been as large as is typical such that, on a seasonally-adjusted basis, food price momentum has increased in December.

That said, as we had first flagged back in December, food prices are expected to be much lower than previously envisaged in the first quarter of 2024 (calendar year). The drop in food prices is not being driven just by volatile vegetable prices correcting from elevated levels. Instead, cereals and pulses have also begun to decline in recent weeks. This has been compounded by a decline in sugar, milk and oil-seed prices making this a broader food inflation softening.

Figure 1: Rice and Pulse inflation
![](images/eede88735efa47b763bc703c905cb696703d092b663531e775c66784692df06f.jpg)

The softening of cereals and pulses inflation — which have been sticky and elevated in recent months — comes as a pleasant surprise, against the backdrop of the patchy and uneven monsoon, which result in a contraction of food production in the Kharif season, according to first estimates (Figure 1). Instead, it owes its genesis to pre-emptive and aggressive administrative actions by the government that have resulted in a sharp increase in pulse imports to offset domestic production shortfalls alongside releasing buffer stocks of cereals and pulses, all of which appear to have contributed to a softening of prices.

With core inflation momentum muted in recent months, the pleasant surprise on food prices has meant that 1Q24 headline CPI inflation is expected in the 4.5-5% range, below what we had previously envisaged and below the RBI<sup>’</sup>s 5.2% inflation forecast for the quarter.

## Nov IP expected to slow to 3.5% oya

Next week we expect IP to print at 3.5% oya decelerating sharply from 11.7% in October. The sharp slowing in November is driven by an unfavorable base effect, just as the large pick up in October was driven by a favorable base. The large shift in base is on account of Diwali festival falling in a different month in 2023 and 2022 respectively.

## Asia Focus: Remittances

Personal transfers or remittances are historically one of the most stable BOP components, showing remarkable resilience in past downturns (Figure 1). While their significance to the region has declined post-GFC, remittances remain important to several economies (Figure 2), namely the Philippines (7% of GDP) and India (3% of GDP).

Figure 1: ISM manufacturing PMI vs. EM Asia net remittances¹
![](images/4fad5ee5b09fa3d254f40df1c5f440243611e478899117910c21141f847e5339.jpg)
Note: Shaded regions denote previous US recessions 1. Simple average of PH, IN, TH, ID

Figure 2: Net Remittances in 2022
![](images/a911466e5ea861fd8e06cd573f6f4a1e9aed5fc087680eb401d2e2d9fbd7ae7e.jpg)

There are several dynamics worth noting among these markets. Remittance inflows are the most stable in the Philippines (Table 1; Figure 3) and may reflect an established norm for overseas diaspora to send home money through the ebbs and flows of the global business cycle.

We also find that remittance flows in the Philippines and India have seen stronger recoveries vis-à-vis Indonesia since the pandemic. This could be a function of overseas worker profiles. The Philippines and India have a greater share of skilled workers in high-income economies such as the US and the GCC (Figure 4) and have likely benefited from more favorable economic conditions (e.g., tight labor market in the US, high oil prices in GCC) in the past few years.

Going forward, the rapid adoption of digitalization is also set to structurally lift remittance flow by reducing transaction costs and improving the ease of transfers. According to the World Bank (see link), India stands out as a market that has seen one of the most significant reductions in transaction costs, and could be a key reason why remittance flows have surged since the pandemic.
